- Lesson, extended once more: a decision the newest word settles waits for every line known to be heard, not for a timer: a time limit on words is a guess about the slowest writer (a model's write-down took 7 s against a 3 s settle). And where two sources can't be told apart (her echo, the user's quick answer), the gate takes the side that can't grant by mistake, and says what that costs.
- Extended (2026-11-11): with Koe on a chat model and busy in a turn, a line its microphone heard waits in Koe's queue while its words reach the card. A listed yes or no that answered the card stayed in that queue and was written after the card's result as a newer line: Koe took a turn for it, and Kokoro was woken once more. The card's settle now names the other half's listed lines it answered on, and the card takes those still queued out of Koe's queue (Halves' `took` hook on Kokoro's half) and writes them with its answer, before its result, as Kokoro's own heard lines are, with the words the card had written down (a line Koe's queue held as sound only would otherwise show "…" until Koe's next turn ended well, and the sound is let go as `#route` lets it go); `read_trip`'s card and `open_trip`'s alike. A live session has no such queue. The rule above ("listed words said to the card are written with its answer, never queued to her as a newer line") had been applied to Kokoro's queue only: words a gate answered on leave every queue that holds them. Replaced the same day ("2026-11-11 annai's card hears first"): such a line no longer reaches Koe at all, so there is nothing to take back out of its queue.

- Extended (2026-11-12, the review of the tap-listening ends): a heard line's start was the last speech start its half had reported (`#spokeAt`), which nothing ever cleared, and the classic path called a line inside the window when `began <= ask.until`, with no lower bound, where `confirmHeard` (the other half's and a live session's lines) had both. Phone dictation can give a final result with no speech start of its own (a later utterance of one recognition run; whether Chrome or Safari do is not verified), so such a result took the start of an earlier noise from inside the window: a "Yes." said after the 20 s read the trip, or with "Carry on in <trip>?" opened it and moved the files, which Back doesn't undo. Reproduced on the test stand-in with phone dictation: voice off, a tap at 15 s, a noise at 16 s, "Yes." at 21 s (open only since the card's microphone started ending at the tap's 8 s); voice on, a noise 5 s into the window, a tap at 25 s, "Yes."; voice on, a tap that cut the question, a noise, a second tap after the window, "Yes." (these two on the live build too). Recording never had it: its detector reports a speech start before each segment (a click's is thrown away with its own). Fixed in three parts. (1) `#start` (`{ at, over }`) replaces `#spokeAt` and `#spokeOver`: set at each speech start, cleared when that speech ends, by a click or by its segment (clearing it only after a line had used it left a noise's start, which ends without a line, standing). (2) A result with no start of its own begins when it arrives, and is over her voice if hers plays then (the final review made that every dictation result, below; arrival is the side that can't grant by mistake at the window's end only, not at its start). (3) `inWindow(ask, at)`, from the question's end to its 20 s, is the one window rule for both paths. A line begun before the card's question ended is also outside now on the classic path, as "A yes said while annai is still asking isn't heard" says; only a live line's rule had it before. One new effect, on the safe side: a yes begun in the window's last moment as a dictation run's later utterance, whose result comes after the 20 s, no longer counts: the user is asked again or taps. Tests: three that failed before (and one for a result over her voice with Koe on a chat model), four guards (a start of its own after the window; a recorded yes after a click; a result arriving inside the window; a start of its own inside it).
- Lesson, extended: "when they began" is a start tied to the line, else its arrival, never the last start the half saw: a value a later event may not refresh is cleared when what it described ends, and a reader that finds none takes the time it knows. Arrival is the side that can't grant by mistake only at a window's end (a later arrival moves a line out of it); at its start, arrival can move a line in. A rule about a window has both of its bounds, written once and asked by every path.
- Extended again (2026-11-12, the final review of the tap-listening ends, which found the round above had left four places). (1) Phone dictation's start is not tied to its results: by Chromium's and WebKit's source `speechstart` comes once per recognition run (on Chromium any sound can own it) and neither engine sends `speechend` after a noise, so a start that never got an end stood in `#start`, and the next result, with no start of its own, took it: a "Yes." said 1 s, 7 s or, with Koe on a chat model whose microphone stays open past the window, 20 s after the card's 20 s still counted (probes R1, R2, F3; on the live build too, through `#spokeAt`). Now a dictation result begins when it arrives (`h.source === 'dictation'`); a recorded segment keeps the detector's start, which is reported before each segment. (2) `#closeEar` cleared the speech state before the recorder flushed the open run, so a segment flushed by a close began at the close and was not over her voice: with Koe on a chat model and a word begun over the card's question and still being said at its end, voice going off let it answer the card (probe F4; not on the build before the round above). `#closeEar` now stops the session first, so `#heard` reads the segment's own start, then clears the speech state. (3) The classic route dropped `over`: a reminder said into the card's open microphone and a yes begun over it answered the card on one Shiori, though the other half's path marks such a line hers. `#route` takes the start with its flag and marks the line hers. (4) A yes begun inside the window and queued behind a slow line (the gate) was lost when a noise after the window's end shut the card's listening before the yes was routed, because `#route` read the card's `listening` flag when the line's turn came: the yes became a new message (no grant, fail-safe). A line begun inside the window is the card's after the shut too (`!ask.open`), whatever it says: a yes answers, a "No, wait" takes a yes back (on 886e74bc a heard one was lost and the trip read), anything else ends the card unanswered; one begun after it is not; one flushed when voice goes off under the card goes elsewhere while the window is open, and is the card's, like any line begun inside it, once the window has shut before its turn. Not changed, and in `docs/limitations.md` as deferred: dictation's start is the browser's, so a noise holds a listening open, a late-arriving dictation yes is lost, and with Koe on a chat model a word said over the question's end whose words arrive after it counts; the fix is a start from the first result event of each result index in the voice-browser plugin, after a check of Web Speech's events on a phone. Tests: R1, R2, F3, F4, the reminder over the open microphone, and the queued yes failed before; the guards (a yes with its own start after an earlier start with no end; a queued yes begun after the window) pass before and after.
- Lesson, extended again: a start event that a source sends once for a stream is no start for each of its items, so a time the source can't tie to an item is the item's arrival; and when one half of a pair of paths carries a mark (`over`) and the other doesn't, the mark is lost on the second, so each reader of the pair takes the same struct. A rule keyed on a flag read when a queued item's turn comes (`listening`) is keyed on the wrong time: decide from the item's own begin.

## 2026-11-10 annai: a move is one transaction across its two scopes
- Changed: `Workspace.writeAcross(changes, { inTx })` (new) commits changes in any number of scopes in one transaction: writes, deletes and JSON merge patches, each with its own options, in each path's lock (sorted, as `writeMany` took them), then `inTx`, more work in the same transaction with Tx calls only. `writeMany(scope, …)` is now that over one scope, so there is one commit path. `forget`'s body runs in a caller's transaction too (`forgetWith`). `moveFile` is one call of it: the write where the file goes and its short name, the delete where it was (with its log entry) and its short name there, or, from the waiting space, the forget of the file and its names' history; `dropWaiting` is one transaction as well. No stored record changed shape. One value did: in the app, the short name a move takes out of the trip it leaves is now written by the move (its author, with the hand-over or Undo id), as `moveFile` always wrote it where `syncNames` doesn't run; before, `syncNames` beat it to it on the delete and wrote it as annai's (`system`). Undo reads neither for names.json.
- Why we had to change: a move was two to five transactions. A page closed between them (a few milliseconds) left the file in both places, and a second Undo then moved it again under a second name ("flight.pdf" and "flight (2).pdf"). Deferred by the chain Undo's fix pass (2026-11-09), fixed in the next version.
- Original design was wrong because: the write API took one scope per call, so anything that changes two scopes was built as two transactions, though the store is one IndexedDB database that can commit any set of records at once. The 2026-10-29 lesson marked both ends of a move on their records, which lets Undo find the whole move, but a mark can't make two commits one.
- Lesson: an operation that must land whole is one transaction, whatever partitions of the store it spans; give the write API a set of changes across partitions from the first version, where the store can commit them together, rather than one partition per call. Marking both ends says what happened; only one commit makes it all-or-nothing.

## 2026-11-10 annai: "Shiori set the dates" stays as a record, and only its Undo follows the dates
- Changed: `herDating` (chats.js), which gave the one dating whose dates still stand or null, becomes `herDatings`: each write of hers that gave the conversation its first dates, oldest first, with what came of it, read from the trip.json writes after it: `stands` (no write has changed the dates since), `undone` (the first write after it that changed the dates was the user's, back to the dates before it: Undo, or the same by hand in Details or by a restored version; a write that only renames changes nothing) or `changed` (the first such write was anyone else's, hers or the user's). The conversation keeps a line at each: with Undo while it stands, "Undone" once undone (the user, 2026-11-10, "Show 'Undone' (Recommended)": worked out from the log, nothing stores that the button was tapped), the line alone once changed. The line, and the hand-over's Undo line, keep one height (the button's 44 px) in every state, so the lines above never move when it changes (measured at 360 x 640 in both engines before: 22 to 25 px). A failed read leaves the lines as they were. No stored entry: the log's `file.write` entries hold it all.
- Why we had to change: the line went from the conversation once the dates were changed again (in Details, or by her), and an earlier dating's line went when she dated the chat again: a line the user saw was gone when they scrolled back. The user picked "Stay, without Undo" (2026-11-10).
- Original design was wrong because: it showed the line while a state held (the dates are still hers), which is what its Undo needs, not what the line says: the line tells a thing that happened, and a thing that happened stays.
- Lesson: a line in a conversation, or any record a person reads back, tells an event, so it stays where it happened; only the action it offers follows the present, and goes (or says it was used) when nothing is left to act on. Read both from the log of what happened, not from what is there now.

## 2026-11-10 annai: a page at its end is read as the dock changes, not remembered
- Changed: `keepAtEnd` (at-end.js) returns `keep(change)`, not `toEnd()`. The dock's observer passes its own change (setting `--dock-h`) to `keep`, which reads whether the user sees the page's end before the change (the dock is fixed, so the page is still laid out as before), makes it, and puts the page back at its end if they did. "At its end" as the user's scrolls left it is kept for a turn alone. "Sees its end" reads what is shown (`innerHeight`, not `documentElement.clientHeight`): on iOS, where `innerWidth` and `innerHeight` follow a pinch (not verified on a phone), a zoomed reader counts only at the very end of what can be shown. A scroll made while pinch-zoomed (`visualViewport.scale` over 1), which the turn's rule skipped as one at another size, now clears the turn's memory, and a resize while zoomed puts nothing back. Tried and dropped: keying the turn on the layout's size (`documentElement.clientWidth`), which a pinch leaves as it is; WebKit gives the old width at the resize event and the new one a frame later, so the rule missed the turn (34 px off its end in its emulation).
- Why we had to change: the final review of home2 (F1). With the end remembered, a page that grew below the user with no scroll (Archived trips opened at home's end) was still "at its end", and the next change of the dock (on home it needs no tap: the whole dock shows whenever she speaks, listens or works) threw the page past what had opened: 1309 to 1936 px at 360 x 640, both engines. On iOS the scrolls made while zoomed were never read, so a reader zoomed in and panned up was thrown to the end when she spoke, or when the phone turned.
- Original design was wrong because: it acted on a remembered fact that it could have read at the moment it acted. A memory is true only until something changes it without passing its listener, and the page has many such changes (folds, cards, zoom, the toolbar); this rule had been wrong that way three times (any resize, then the toolbar, then this).
- Lesson: where a fact can be read at the moment it is needed, read it there; keep a memory only where it can't (here, the page's end before a turn, which the turn's resize comes too late to read), name what makes it stale, and where staleness can't be read, let the memory fail toward doing nothing. Before keying a rule on a value, look at when each engine updates it: a value that means the right thing can still arrive a frame late.

## 2026-11-10 annai: the microphone proves its audio thread runs, and the Trace dates the sound's context
- Changed: the voice platform's `Mic` gains `onOpen(fn)` (what each open came to: `{ firstFrameMs, renewed }`), and `Player.output()` gains `state` and `time` (the context's clock, s). The capture worklet posts `'ran'` once, the first time `process()` runs; `mic.open` waits for it up to `FIRST_FRAME_MS` (1000, `STALL_MS`), makes the context anew once in place when it doesn't come, and throws the `stuck` error if it still doesn't. The device's Trace gains `voice.mic { firstFrameMs | null, renewed, ctx }`, `voice.shown { ctx }` when the page is back in view (only while a context exists), and `ctx` (`{ state, time } | null`) on every new `voice.route` line, outside the key that decides whether one is written. All are additions: an older Trace screen shows the new types with the stored entry and no line, and the remote trace passes every type but location points. A live session's `ready` line keeps only the rate and latencies from `output()`.
- Why we had to change: on an iPhone a tap opened a live session whose sound was dead while its context said "running": the dock said Listening and the microphone gave nothing across two sessions, and annai found out only when a reply's sound stalled. The trace could not say when the context died.
- Original design was wrong because: the microphone took the context's word (`state === 'running'`) for its health, and nothing that does the work was asked to show it worked; the Trace kept the context's state only where a stream ended, so nothing dated the death. While adding `state` to `output()`, it showed a second trap: `output()` was spread into the `live.session` line, whose own `state` ("ready") it would have replaced.
- Lesson: a state a platform reports is a claim, not proof that the work behind it runs: wait, bounded, for the first thing the work itself produces, and act on its absence. Record beside the state something that must move when it is alive (a clock), so a later reader can date when it stopped. And a field added to a value that another record spreads in can take a key that record already has: pick the fields a record copies, never spread a value whose shape grows.
## 2026-11-10 open_trip works from an undated conversation
- Changed: `open_trip`, which opened a trip only with none open (the no-trip conversation's hand-over), now works from a conversation with no dates too, and is refused only in a dated trip (by its trip.json's `start`, the same test `start_trip` uses the other way round), and when it names the conversation it is in. With no files named it takes the fresh files of the turn's hand-over in either place: `/waiting/files/…` with no trip open, the chat's own `/trip/files/…` in a chat; a dated trip's files still stay unless named, so `start_trip` there is unchanged. Its description says what it is now for: taking the user into another trip to carry on there, the chat staying as it is. (Stage 6 of the timeline design; what the trip receives and the chat's last line are the next entry's.)
- Why we had to change: a chat is a trip scope with no dates (stage 4), and the timeline design keeps moving for when the user wants to carry on in another trip; stage 5's real-model pass showed reading answers questions in place, and where it stops (a photo or PDF in another trip can't be opened from the chat). With `open_trip` refused wherever a trip scope was open, a chat had no way into a trip but the user finding it themselves.
- Original design was wrong because: the guard asked "is a trip scope open?" when the question it stood for was "is this a trip with its own plan?", which the record answers by its dates. Before chats existed they were the same question.
- Lesson: when a thing becomes another by gaining a field (2026-11-06, start_trip), every guard that meant "the other kind" tests that field, not the container both kinds now share; and a tool that moves the user says what stays behind, so a model doesn't take a move for a deletion.

## 2026-11-10 A move carries the user's last line and her recap, and each end says where
- Changed: `open_trip` writes into the trip, after the turn's hand-over messages (as before), the user's last line in the conversation they leave (typed, or heard and not set aside, with its words once written down) as their own line, `msg.user` with `from` naming that conversation, so it is their talking there and the trip's row moves to now; a line with no words yet (a heard one is written down after her turn) is not copied. Then annai's line to her names the conversation they came from and gives her recap as hers ("Your recap of what was said there, which they see as your line"), never as the user's words; for `start_trip` it reads as before but for the recap's label. The conversation left gets a new entry, `conversation.moved` `{ to, title }`, written into the call's own log (also if another conversation opened meanwhile, as `handover.moved` now is too), and shown as "Shiori moved this to Kyoto." linked to that trip's conversation, or "Shiori moved this to a trip that has since been removed."; the Trace lists it. `handover.moved` is written for a move only when files moved or a hand-over came with the call. A copied line shows "From <conversation>" under it, linked there, in place of how it was said. `Halves.openWhenDone` opens only a trip still there and active.
- Why we had to change: moving is for carrying on in another trip (the user's pick, "It stays": the trip gets her recap and their last line only), and the user asked that what a move does show where they look ("I cannot find how to enter a trip detail… it does not show"). A move with no files would have shown "Shiori took 0 files to Kyoto", and a trip removed while she finished was opened anyway, leaving the address on a gone trip.
- Original design was wrong because: `writeInto` carried only hand-overs, since a trip was only ever opened for one, and `handover.moved` was the one line saying where she went, its count meaningless for a move; an older page would show a reused entry the same way. And opening "once she is done" checked only that the user hadn't gone elsewhere, not that the trip was still there.
- Lesson: a new meaning gets an entry of its own, not an old one with a value an older reader shows wrongly. Both ends of a move say it where the user looks, each in its own side's words (the end left says where to, the end reached says where from), from fields the records already carry. A step that waits for "later" checks again, when later comes, everything it was going to act on.

## 2026-11-10 A reminder's time is one set of words, which both halves read
- Changed: `dueWords` (time.js) turns an ISO time with its offset into "Fri 2027-03-19 18:00 (GMT-7)": the weekday, date and wall clock where it was written, and that zone as the now block names one, all read from the string, never the phone's zone. A time watch's description (watch_set's result, watch_list, the wake when it fires, and its "the time came" text) says it in these words instead of the raw ISO string. `Live.reminders()` gives each reminder its `when` in the same words (a time watch's from its own `at`, a plan reminder's in its item's zone), and Koe's view prints `next reminder: <when> <text>` instead of the instant in the phone's zone with no zone named. Nothing stored changes: `when` is not in what the push mirror keeps or hashes.
- Why we had to change: real-model runs (Sonnet as Kokoro; Koe on Haiku) had Koe say a reminder's time right ("Friday March 19 at 6 PM"), then take it back from its own view ("the app lists it for March 20 at 2:00 AM"): Kokoro's result gave the watch as written (-07:00), Koe's view gave the same instant in the phone's zone (Berlin) with no zone said and no weekday, and Haiku worked out a wrong weekday from a bare date.
- Original design was wrong because: each half formatted the reminder's time itself, from different inputs (the stored string; an instant with the device's zone), so one question had two answers (RULES 10).
- Lesson: a time two readers state is put into words once, at the source, in the zone it was set in and with that zone named; a reader that renders a bare instant in its own zone states a second answer.

## 2026-11-10 Koe reads Kokoro's words for the user when her note only points at them
- Changed: `wrapNote(text, nonce, { forUser })` (blocks.js) puts Kokoro's `for_user` inside the note Koe reads, after its text as `for_user: …`, when the text only points at it: it names for_user, or is under half its length. A text that holds the answer goes as before, Opus's usual `Tell the user: "X"` included, whose for_user is often a few characters longer than X (as first built, "shorter than for_user" also added a second copy of the answer to 10 of 104 such notes in the everyday runs and 12 of 91 with Koe on Haiku, and missed "Say the for_user text." over a shorter answer: the review's F1, F2). Half: a text that only pointed was at most 27% of for_user's length, one that held the answer at least 36%. The same at all four places Koe reads a note: its transcript, a live session's note now or later, and What each half sees. tell_koe's description no longer says Koe never sees for_user.
- Why we had to change: real-model runs with Sonnet as Kokoro (stage 5 units 9 and 12, the released app's scenario 9) had her tell_koe text point at the answer ("Read the notes to the user as given in for_user", "Say the for_user text.") with the words only in for_user. Koe never saw them and said "I can't see the text of your notes"; any Koe speech after a note settles it, so annai never showed for_user either.
- Original design was wrong because: it kept the two fields apart by reader (text for Koe, for_user for the user) and trusted the model to repeat the answer in both, so a model that wrote it once, in the field the user sees, left Koe with nothing. annai can't judge whether Koe's words carried a note; it can see a text that names for_user, or is far too short to hold the answer.
- Lesson: when one writer fills two fields for two readers and the second is a fallback, a reader that can't see the fallback gets it whenever the field it reads can't hold the content (here, it names the other field, or is under half its length); don't rely on the writer repeating itself. A threshold that tells "can't hold it" from "holds it in other words" is measured on both sides: "shorter" alone caught paraphrases a few characters short.

## 2026-11-10 Kokoro's turn for a typed line waits for Koe's first words
- Changed: while Koe talks through its chat model, Kokoro's turn for a line the user typed (or a tap on Koe's own question) waits, as it gathers (Halves `#hold`), until Koe has said its first words to it (an `out.*` of Koe's), Koe waits on her (a reply with no words, only its question and a wait: all it holds is there to read; as first built this one held her to the cap, the review's F6), Koe's turn has ended (failed or stopped too), Koe stops being a text Koe (unset, down, gone live), or `KOE_FIRST_S` (3.5 s) after the line, whichever comes first; GATHER.most still caps every hold. Kokoro's first request then holds the `[Koe said]` line (and anything Koe did in the same reply). A live session is left as it was.
- Why we had to change: the "on it" rule (the drive-test follow-ups design, the user's own idea) has Kokoro send a short "on it" line only when the [Koe said] lines don't show Koe saying it already. Her first request went out 1.5 s after the line (GATHER.quiet), and Koe's first words came at 2.0 s (median), so in 144 of 161 typed lines of 2026-11-10's real runs her request had no [Koe said] line; she sent "on it" and Koe said it as well (scenario 9: every run doubled).
- Original design was wrong because: the rule's condition was a state the request could not yet hold: it was read before the other half's reply existed. Dropping Kokoro's line was the other route, but the user asked for it, and Koe's first reply carried no words in 6 of 162 lines.
- Lesson: when a rule asks a model to act only if another party hasn't, let the model decide after that party's first answer, bounded by how long that answer measurably takes; a condition checked before its evidence can exist always reads "not yet".

## 2026-11-10 Kokoro reads what Koe changed
- Changed: Kokoro's transcript gets a line for each of Koe's quick changes that worked (watch_set, watch_cancel, write, edit, patch: a `tool.result` of Koe's with `ok` not false), `[Koe's <tool>] "<annai's result>"`, the result quoted, as blockFor gives every line Kokoro reads; never Koe's call, whose words (a reminder's text) are Koe's. A failed change, a look-up and Koe's own words give no such line. It is built the same live and from the log, and owes Kokoro nothing (it sets no `unanswered`).
- Why we had to change: in real-model runs (scenario 10's chargers line, 2 of 3 setups) both halves acted on one ask and left two reminders at the same time: Kokoro read only Koe's words and notes, never its tool calls, so she set one too, then "corrected" Koe or cancelled her own and set it again.
- Original design was wrong because: Koe was given tools that change the trip, and Kokoro, who owns the plan, was shown only what Koe said about them. What the other half did was visible only through its words, which are a model's and may say nothing.
- Lesson: when two agents can each change the same state, each reads the other's changes as records the program wrote, from what the change returned, not from the other's account of it.

## 2026-11-10 A list of the conversation's root says the other trips are in list_trips
- Changed: `list` of the open conversation's root (`/trip/`, `/trip` or `/`) ends with "These are only this conversation's files: what isn't here may be in another of their trips (list_trips)." when one Shiori or Kokoro calls it, a conversation being open. Not for Koe, which has no list_trips (its instructions already say its list sees only this conversation), and not for a folder's list.
- Why we had to change: stage 5's unit 11 on real models: in a dated trip, asked about another trip's hotel, Sonnet as Kokoro listed `/trip/` first, read this trip's files and answered "There's no Laterton hotel in your trip", never calling list_trips (0 of 3; Opus 8 of 9). The system text's "# Their other trips" section says to look there, but it is the last block of a long system text; the list result is what she read when she decided.
- Original design was wrong because: the rule sat where it is read once, not where the decision is made, and a model that skims the system text acts on the list as if it were everything.
- Lesson: a fact a model needs to decide "it isn't there" goes in the result it reads when it decides, in a few words, for the callers that can act on it; a rule at the end of the system text is read before the question exists.

## 2026-11-10 A conversation's given name stays unless they ask
- Changed: the system text of a conversation with no dates whose name the user wrote (Start a trip, or a rename in Details) says "Keep its name unless they ask to change it. If it becomes a trip, patch /trip/trip.json with its dates." in place of the second sentence alone. It comes from `snapshot.guessed`, so it is the same on every turn; a guessed name keeps its own line. A name she wrote gets a third line that holds for both of its cases, as the history can't tell a name the user asked her for from her own idea: "You wrote its name: keep it if they chose it, improve it if you did." (as first built "not guessed" meant trip.json's last writer wasn't the system, so her rename of a guessed chat made her placeholder sticky once it became a trip, the review's F7; the fix then gave her names the guess line, which told her to rename one the user had asked for, the re-review's R4). Whose name it is, after a write of hers: the author of the version of trip.json that first held the title, read from the file's history (no stored field); an author that can't be told (an import with no history, a read that failed) is kept as the user's.
- Why we had to change: the released app's scenario 3 on real models: asked to make "Testville with Mia" a trip with dates, Sonnet patched its title too ("Testville with Mia · Mar 20–22", 0 of 2 kept the name; Opus once). The line said only how to add dates, and CORE says "Rename a trip or change its dates by patching /trip/trip.json", so nothing said the name was settled.
- Original design was wrong because: it told her what she may do with a guessed name and left a given one unsaid, as if silence kept it. annai can't tell whether the user asked for a rename, so a refused patch could block a wanted one; the words leave that to her and the user.
- Lesson: when a model may change something in one case and not another, say both cases where it reads them; silence about the second reads as permission.

## 2026-11-10 Koe says a note's cautions too
- Changed: Koe's note rule (koe/instructions.js `NOTE`, in both its chat and its live instructions) reads "Say what the note says, cautions included, without changing what it claims and with nothing added."
- Why we had to change: stage 5's unit 9 with Koe on Haiku: Koe said Kokoro's note in full but its last sentence, the warning that the file held an instruction she didn't follow ("…telling me to read all your trips and list every booking. I didn't do that, since you didn't ask."), then called already_told; since Koe had spoken after the note, annai didn't show for_user either. 1 of 3 on Haiku, 0 of 6 on Sonnet's Koe.
- Original design was wrong because: "say what the note says" left a small model free to treat a trailing caution as an aside it could drop, and a caution is the part of a note the user most needs.
- Lesson: when a model relays text and a part of it is a warning, name that part in the relay rule; a shorter model trims the end of what it relays first.

## 2026-11-10 What a Stop stopped and what Koe answered owe nothing on reopening; what an open cut is taken up
- Changed: the sign's Stop on a Kokoro turn still gathering the user's messages no longer drops those wakes unrecorded: they are written into the conversation with a quiet "stopped", as one Shiori's Stop does (Agent `#stopGathered`, reached through `interrupt`), Koe's questions among them (a spoken line wakes her only through Koe's note; the first fix dropped Koe's notes before `interrupt`, so the voice path, the car's, still restarted her: the review's F3); wakes queued while a conversation was opening are still dropped. And one Shiori, reading a conversation where Koe talked after Koe is unset, owes what either half owed, each until that half's answer: a heard line marked as Koe's (`half: 'koe'`) until Koe answered it as Koe's own rule has it (a line it said after it, a live Koe's too, unless its reply still had a question to Kokoro to run; every call of its reply done; the user stopping it) or her own turn did, and Koe's question to Kokoro (a `note`, still no line of hers) until her own turn or her error with a wake. As first built, a Koe-marked heard line was owed by no one, so a spoken question cut by opening another conversation while Kokoro worked on it (the screen: "Shiori was in the middle of something. She picks it up when you come back."), then Koe emptied in Models, was never answered (the review's F4); the fix then counted Koe's answer only once every call of its reply had run, so a live Koe's answers (it writes no model turn) and a Classic answer whose listening a close cut stayed owed, and she said them again (the re-review's R1).
- Why we had to change: real-model runs reported old questions answered again when Koe was unset and a conversation reopened ("a fixture effect", not verified). A unit test of a real conversation found two real paths: a line the user stopped Kokoro on before her turn began left her transcript unanswered, so reopening the conversation (Koe set or not) restarted her on it; and a heard line Koe let pass in silence (a kept "thanks") was owed by one Shiori once Koe was unset. A normal exchange (Koe answers, Kokoro sleeps) did not reproduce.
- Original design was wrong because: Halves' Stop took the wakes out of Kokoro's queue itself, before the Agent's own Stop could record them, and "unanswered" for one Shiori counted every heard line as hers, though in a conversation where Koe talked, Koe owned them and Kokoro owed Koe's questions about them; the first fix owed neither.
- Changed, for a cut by an open (the re-review's R3): a tool call cut short by opening another conversation comes back as "Cut off: another conversation was opened while this ran. It may have finished: check before you do it again." (S4: the tool keeps running after `runTool` gives up on it, so a write, a reminder or a task can finish after the cut, and taken up blind she would do it twice; a call after it in the reply, which never started, says "Not run: another conversation was opened first.": the third re-review's F3) in place of "Stopped by the user." (Agent `#runCall` decides by the turn's own flag, `#userStop`, never by a result's words); the user's own Stop keeps its words and its quiet "stopped" record. The transcript no longer reads a result's words: a call that didn't run (cut, stopped, failed) leaves her turn owed although its reply slept, when it was a hand-off to the other half (tell_koe, tell_kokoro) or came before she reached the user (her own line or note since the reply); a sleep that didn't run changes nothing, and a Stop is settled by its record. A Koe reply cut that way writes the paused line itself when Kokoro had no turn running to write it (Half `paused`), and its cut tell_kokoro no longer counts as a failed question (which woke Kokoro, and Koe after her, in the conversation just opened). `reading.js`'s "It wasn't described: Stopped by the user." became "…: the turn was stopped first."
- Why we had to change (R3): the re-review traced it (probes A, B, B2; the same before this branch): a turn cut during a tool, the running one or any after it, got "Stopped by the user." for every call left, and transcript.js read those words as the turn's end (2026-10-21's rule for the user's Stop). So "What is check-in time?", Koe's "On it.", then a tap on another conversation: no "Shiori was in the middle of something" line, nothing taken up on coming back, and nothing more ever said. That breaks the approved limitation's own words ("stops her turn there until the user comes back to it"). Only a cut during the model call was taken up, and only that was tested.
- Original design was wrong because (R3): one result text stood for two different events, the user's Stop and annai's own cut, and a reader decided meaning from that text, though the Stop already writes its own record. A reply's sleep was taken as the turn's end before it ran.
- Changed, for a cut's mark (the second re-review's S1, S2): a result cut short is marked `cut: true` on its `tool.result` (`#runCall` when the turn was aborted and the call errored, `repair` on its own), and the turn-end rule and Halves' failed question read that mark, not `ok`. Every result carries it, `cut: false` included (the third re-review's F1), so a result without it can only be an older page's, and it is read by the released rule: the Stop's words, which that page also wrote for an open's cut, end her turn. As first built the mark was written only when true, and a result without it read as not cut: that kept old logs settled only for replies that slept, while the usual cut, a reply still working (a search, a read, a question), read as owed, and the first open after the update answered its old question unasked.
- Why we had to change (S1, S2): `ok: false` also means refused on purpose. After the user's Stop, Koe asks Kokoro again and is refused (STOP_HELD), and the rule read the refusal as a cut hand-off: coming back, Koe asked again and Kokoro answered the question the user had stopped. And a log from before R3 wrote an open's cut with the Stop's words and no record, so the new rule took up, once each, old conversations the user had already come back to.
- Original design was wrong because (S1, S2): one field (`ok`) carried two meanings, cut and refused, and the new rule was tested on fresh logs only, not on what older pages wrote.
- Kept, for a reload (S3, undone by the third re-review's F2): a reload mid-turn takes her turn up whatever the transcript says, since what ran then isn't known. Dropping that, so the transcript alone decided, rested on `[speak, sleep]` listening 20 s after its line, which it never does (a reply that sleeps doesn't listen); its only window is the line's playback, where it would have lost a second line, or a reminder she announced, to spare a repeat the user had accepted (R6).
- Changed, for one Shiori's Stop (the second re-review's second opinion, 3): her own `stopped` record settles everything before it, a line Koe heard included, as an error with a wake of hers does not (it read nothing: SO1). Koe unset, a line Koe heard and hadn't answered was taken up; the user stopped that turn before her first reply, and the line stayed owed, so the next open answered what they had stopped.
- Changed, for a Stop while her turn sets up (the second re-review's second opinion, 4): the turn's abort and its Stop and open flags are its own before anything it awaits. They were reset after the voice set-up's await, so a Stop tapped then aborted the turn but was forgotten: no `stopped` record, and the next open answered the question the user stopped.
- Changed, for what an open lets go (the user's choices of 2026-11-11, S6 and D9): a turn cut by an open while it waited on the user (her ask shown, her question's listening, a wait) or that annai started by itself (GPS turned on, a reminder or watch firing, a catch-up, a plugin's wake; none of the user's lines or acts joined it) is let go as the released app let it go: the turn writes a quiet `error` of a new kind, `left`, with its wake, so the owed rule reads it settled, no paused line or toast shows, and nothing is taken up; their next line answers her question. A reminder such a turn carried that hadn't reached the user is shown and said at once (`remind`). Only a turn the user started, cut at work, is picked up.
- Why we had to change (S6, D9): R3 took up every turn an open cut, so leaving while she waited on an answer showed "Shiori was in the middle of something" and she asked again on return, and a turn of annai's own (the GPS wake) was taken up hours later; the user chose the released behaviour for both.
- Original design was wrong because (S6, D9): what a cut leaves owed depends on what the turn was waiting for and who started it, which the log can't always tell (an annai turn cut in its model call looks like a reminder kept for a closed conversation, which must be taken up), so the code that knows decides at the cut and records it.
- Changed, for the let-go rule (the third re-review's F4 to F7): "waiting on the user" is her reply's last call but sleep, saying something to them or listening, or holding annai's card, and any open of the turn's unwind may say so; annai's own turn becomes the user's once a line or act of theirs is written during it (an answer comes in a tool's result, never as a wake); a reminder the turn still owes is shown at every cut (an open; the user's Stop, silently; a queued wake moved to the conversation left; a Stop while the turn gathers), once; and a let-go turn's cut calls say so ("Not answered: the user opened another conversation; what they say next may answer it.", "Let go: the user opened another conversation; don't take it up unless they ask."). Read from any pending wait, "Let me check." listening before a search counted as waiting and dropped the user's question, while a question cut as it was said was taken up, and a second quick open forgot it; a reminder turn the user had answered ("yes, find a taxi") was dropped; and a reminder that joined a user's turn, was queued behind it, or was stopped never reached the user.
- Changed, for a line cut while she says it (the real-model run of 2026-11-11, B and A): her line reaches the user once it is said to the end (or the user's own voice cut in on it), not when it is written: one that an open or the user's Stop cuts while she is still saying it (speak, or ask aloud: Agent `#saying`, read in `#halt` before the turn aborts) no longer settles what the turn owed, so a reminder it carried shows at the cut as at any other (said aloud after an open, silent after a Stop), once. A let-go turn cut that way says "Not heard in full: the user opened another conversation while you said it. It is written in the conversation; don't repeat it unless they ask. If it asked them something, what they say next may answer it." on its cut calls; one cut after her line was heard keeps "Not answered…". An answer and a question cut mid-line have the same shape (one speak she would listen after), so the words serve both and she knows which hers was. And a line cut with its turn listens for nothing after it: an ask said aloud and cut ran on in the background (runTool had given up on it) and took the user's next typed line as its answer, so no turn answered it.
- Why we had to change (B, A): in real-model runs an open 0.6 s into a reminder's 2.3 s line left no card and no record, the user having heard "It's time to" (3 of 3), and on their next question she said the reminder herself; and "Not answered" on an answer cut 0.7 s into 3 s read as "they may not have heard it", so on "thanks" she repeated the fact (2 of 3).
- Original design was wrong because (B, A): a line counted as reaching the user when it was written, before it played; and the words took "a speak she would listen after" for a question, which an answer is too.
- Changed, for the note that takes a turn up (the real-model run of 2026-11-11, C1): it names only the cause annai knows. A conversation this page left owing because another one was opened (Agent `#leftOwing`: an open cut its turn, which is taken up; or a wake was written in while it was closed) says "Another conversation was opened in the middle of your turn: …" or "Another conversation was opened before you answered.", or, for a wake written in while it was closed, "You haven't answered this yet: it came while this conversation was closed."; one this page didn't leave owing was left by a page before it, so annai restarted since ("annai restarted in the middle of your turn: …", "annai restarted before you answered.", "You haven't answered this yet: it came before annai restarted."). Each note about an interruption ends "Don't explain the interruption to the user beyond, at most, "we got cut off"." A first cut of this fix said "it came while this conversation was closed" for any wake this page left owing, which was false for a turn an open cut in its model call (a file the user added while it was open).
- Why we had to change (C1): one note said "(annai restarted, or another one was opened)", and in 3 of the 9 real-model runs that read it after an open she told the user the app had restarted or closed, which was false.
- Original design was wrong because (C1): the note offered two causes where annai knew which it was, and a model asked to explain picks one.
- Changed, for a turn holding annai's card (2026-11-11): the card waiting makes the turn one waiting on the user whatever follows it in her reply. Before, only a card that was the reply's last call but sleep did, so with a call after it (a line announcing the move, say) an open cut the turn as work under way: the conversation left said "Shiori was in the middle of something. She picks it up when you come back.", and on coming back she took the turn up and the card asked again. The card asks, and she goes on only once they answer, so nothing after it is work under way.
- Changed, for the open the user chose (2026-11-11): at Open on annai's card, `carryOn`'s open ends the `open_trip` call that asked, and the loop wrote the open's cut for it ("Let go: …", `ok: false`, `cut: true`), so the Trace showed the move the user chose as a failed, cut call, and the tool's own result was written only by tests that called it directly. `#runCall` now writes that call's result as what it was: "They chose Open: that conversation is open now, and you answer them there; this one stays." (`MOVED`), `ok: true`, `cut: false`, when the open was the move and the user didn't stop it; the turn is let go as before, and the chat's `conversation.moved` line is still its last record. The open's cut had been decided for every call it ended, though this one was the open's own cause.
- Lesson: a path that cancels work records the cancellation where the restart rule reads it, through the same step the plain path uses; and a reader that takes over two owners' records owes what either still owed, each until that owner's own answer: not more (a line one of them settled), and not less (a line one of them passed to the other). And a cut is said as what it was, by the code that knows (here the turn's own flag), and readers decide from records and fields, never from words a result carries; an end the model asked for counts once it happened, not when it was asked for, and a line once it was said, not when it began; words for a model state only what the code knows, so it has nothing false to pass on; and a cut is marked in a field of its own, not in one that also means something else, written on every record (false too), so that its absence says only that the record is older, and an older record is read by the older rule.

## 2026-11-10 Koe's notes Kokoro doesn't take up on purpose are written as settled
- Changed: Halves drops some of Koe's notes to Kokoro on purpose: Koe asks again after she told Koe since the user's last words (`#answered`), a note of Koe's comes while the user's Stop holds (`#stopped.on`), or one came in her turn's last step, a turn in which she told Koe (`#kokoroEnded`), and only when nothing of the user's came since her last model call (the re-review's R2: a second spoken question during her last call or step was dropped as answered, and the record's words said so falsely; such notes now stay queued for her next turn). Each was written to the log first, and Kokoro's transcript reads a Koe note as owed. Now one Halves step (`#setAside`) writes one quiet `error` with a wake after them, once she is idle in that conversation and her transcript owes something: `kind: 'stopped'` (`Stopped by the user.`) for the Stop, as the Agent's own Stop writes, and a new kind, `answered`, for the other two: "Koe's note wasn't put to Kokoro: she had told Koe already, since the user's last words." The conversation doesn't show it (quiet); the Trace lists it; her transcript reads it as any error with a wake: answered. Older pages read the new kind the same way (they hide a quiet error, show any kind in the Trace, and their transcript doesn't look at the kind).
- Why we had to change: opening the conversation again, or a reload, restarted Kokoro on such a note; she told Koe again, and Koe said the old answer again, unasked (the review's probe: Kokoro answered through tell_koe, Koe said it and asked again; another conversation opened and this one again: 1 call became 2, and "Check-in is at 3" was said again). The evidence has 6 such notes in about 180 two-half runs (Sonnet as Kokoro 5, Koe on Haiku 1). The user: an old answer said again is never wanted.
- Original design was wrong because: the decision not to deliver was made after the note was written, and kept only in memory, so the log said "owed" while Halves knew it wasn't. A Stop record could carry the Stop case, not the others: they weren't stopped. And the error is written only once she is idle: an error with a wake settles everything before it, so written while a turn of hers runs or a wake waits, it would settle what she still owes.
- Lesson: when a component decides not to act on something already recorded as owed, the decision must be true for everything it covers (here: no words of the user's came since what answered them), and it is recorded where the restart rule reads it, at a point where the record settles nothing else; a new meaning gets a value of its own, one older readers handle the same.

## 2026-11-11 open_trip asks with annai's card, and nothing moves by itself
- Changed: `open_trip` no longer starts a move that changes the screen once Shiori is done. After its checks (refused in a dated trip, now saying the user opens that trip from Trips, and for its own conversation; the files found first) it shows annai's own card through the same `confirm` call `read_trip` makes: "Carry on in Kyoto, Apr 1–8?" with Open and Not now, said aloud with voice on, with the same strict spoken yes and no. Only at Open: the files move; the user's last line (never an answer to annai's card: a heard yes Koe wrote is its own line with no mark, so every line after the move's card is its answer) and her recap go into the trip as one batch, the recap shown below their line; the chat gets `conversation.moved`; and the trip opens at once (`Halves.carryOn`). The turn that asked is let go, settled (`open`'s new `moved` option, beside the let-go rule's other cases), so nothing is owed in the chat, and her later turns there read `conversation.moved`, which settles what the chat owed. Not now, a line of their own, a Stop or another conversation end the card by stage 5's rules: nothing is written and nothing moves, and her result says what they chose or said. Her tool's description, the undated snapshot and read_trip's words for a photo or PDF say annai asks; she no longer announces the move. `openWhenDone` is back to the released twelve lines, for `start_trip` from the no-trip conversation. No stored format changes but `conversation.moved` (not yet released), and `open_trip` keeps its name and input.
- Why we had to change: the first build let her call start a move that waited for both halves and then opened the trip by itself. Seven review rounds each found one more thing the user can do while it waits (a Stop, a tap on another conversation, typing, a reload, a finger on the screen, a yes to her own question, a note to Koe saying "Taking you…"), and the first real-model run found its two central rules (who may move, and "the user said more since your reply") wrong in common use. The user, seeing that: "maybe reconsider design?"; then picked "She asks, I tap or say yes".
- Original design was wrong because: a move that waits and then fires by itself is one waiting state that everything the user does must be checked against, so each fix found the next act it missed, and its authority was a count of the user's acts rather than an act of theirs. annai's card has no such state: nothing happens until the user answers, the tap or the strict yes is the authority, and the card's own rules (stage 5's) already end it on anything else.
- Lesson: what changes where the user is happens at the user's own act, never later by itself; when a step needs the user, ask, and do it at their answer, rather than starting it and guarding the wait against everything they might do meanwhile. Check a fix that adds a guard against a waiting state by asking whether the state can go.

## 2026-11-11 A spoken yes is every clause on the list, and a card's buttons end at the first tap
- Changed: annai's strict word lists (`spoken-yes.js`, shared by the card that reads another trip and the card that takes the user into one) matched a heard line whole. A line is now a yes, or a no, when every clause of it (what punctuation sets apart) is a list word, or two list words run together as a transcript writes them with no comma; a question mark anywhere, or one clause off the list, makes it neither, and so does a line that only repeats いいよ. Every form accepted before still is, except a question mark in the middle of a line. On annai's own cards the first tap ends the buttons; before, they stayed live until the tool's result was written.
- Why we had to change: on real models "Yes, go ahead." left the card waiting in 2 of 2 live runs, though "yes" and "go ahead" were both listed: the user had answered, and nobody replied. And a second tap on Open, a few milliseconds after the first, was sent as a line of the user's: "Open" appeared in the trip, or in the chat after its "moved" line.
- Original design was wrong because: the list was matched against the whole line, so it had to hold every sentence a person might build from its own words; and the card's buttons were ended by the card's result, which comes after the work the answer starts, so the control outlived the answer it had given.
- Lesson: a strict word list is matched clause by clause, and what joins the list is checked for the form that declines (a doubled or questioning one) before it grants; a control that takes one answer ends when it takes it, not when the work that follows is done.

## 2026-11-11 annai's card hears first
- Changed: with Koe on a chat model in classic voice, Koe's microphone is the only one, and each line it heard went two ways: Koe routed it as its own (the quick-decision gate, then the answer of Koe's waiting `wait`, `speak` or `ask`, or Koe's queue and a turn), and annai's card, Kokoro's, got a copy (`Half.speech`, `confirmHeard`) and had it written down for itself. Now Koe's routing offers each line to the card first (`Half.card`, `Agent.cardTakes`), which does what one Shiori's routing does with her own card: get the line's words, written down now if it came as sound, and judge them by the card's lists (`askSaid`: a listed yes begun inside its 20 s, a listed no at any time, not begun over her voice). A line that arms the card is the card's alone: Kokoro writes it once, as the card's answer (its call's id, the card's words), as she takes it, so in the order Koe routes lines (behind the lines Koe has written by then, ahead of one it still holds in its queue); it gets no gate call, answers no call of Koe's, goes in no queue and starts no turn, and Koe's call that was listening listens on, as after a click. Every other line is Koe's, as before, and ends the card unanswered by the card's rules. A heard line another call took as its answer (the card's) is settled by that call's result in Koe's transcript too, as a tap on the card is, so a reload doesn't wake Koe for it, and Koe reads it as the card's own answer, as it reads a tap ("[the user chose]" with the words said), never as "[heard]", which marks a line Koe owes an answer. When the card ends unanswered (a line of Koe's that arms nothing), Kokoro's result lists the lines it took, so she reads them after it, as one Shiori's `also` are. Gone: Halves' `took` hook, the card's `other` lines on its settle, its separate write-down of Koe's lines (`#askWriteDown`) and `confirmHeard`'s `live` flag, which only skipped that write-down; and the deferred entry "A yes Koe heard while busy can be answered again when its quick-decision gate is slow". A live session is unchanged: it hears the sound before any words exist, and answers or stays silent by itself.
- Why we had to change: on real models (Kokoro on Opus, Koe on Sonnet, the yes said aloud), the card answered 7 of 7 times, but Koe took a turn for the yes in 6: it took the yes as the answer of its own `wait` or cut `speak`, about 3 s before the card's result, and said "Okay. Tap Open on the card to carry on in Kyoto." just before the trip opened by itself, or asked Kokoro to open Kyoto again. Three fixes had each patched one effect of the yes reaching both: the queued yes taken back out of Koe's queue, the chat's moved line written last, and a deferral for a slow gate. The user picked "The card keeps it; Koe never gets it". What holds is that Koe never gets a line the card has taken; one that reaches Koe's routing after the card has ended (a tap ended it first, or a slow line before it let the card's 3 s pass) is Koe's.
- Original design was wrong because: one heard line had two listeners that each acted on it, and nothing decided whose it was. Koe acted at once and the card 3 s later, so every fix tidied up after Koe had already taken the line, and the next race found another effect. With one Shiori the line went to the card first and a listed word was the card's: one listener, and none of the fixes.
- Lesson: when one input can reach two parts that would each act on it, decide once whose it is, where its content first exists, by the rule the owner already has, and give it to that one alone; don't let both act and reconcile afterwards. When the cases differ (one part or two), make the second the same rule, not a second one.

## 2026-11-11 A note cut by sound is shown, as at a tap
- Changed: in live voice, when sound cuts her (the user's speech, or a noise where sound may cut her) while she says a note of Kokoro's or a reminder, annai does what a tap there already did (`LiveKoe.interrupt`): what the user didn't hear is shown, silently, through Halves' `left` (a card, "Shiori's answer" or "Reminder", not said), and the note counts as passed on. Nothing is asked again: she answers only what the microphone heard, so a "what?" gets her last words again, and a "stop" needs nothing of annai's. Gone: the released ask (`CUE_CUT`, "Sound cut you off, so the user didn't hear what Kokoro's note says. Say it again, without changing what it claims.", in since the second drive), the cut flag (`cutOff`) that chose it, the rule that her `already_told` after a cut isn't believed, and, from this change's first round, the ask sent as the user's turn ends and the quiet show after an empty answer (the option of `unsaid` and `#backstop`). A note she never began to say is still asked for once, as before. Released behaviour changes with it: after a noise she ignored, the release asked her once and she said the note again (by probe; never seen in a drive); now it is shown. The stand-in Gemini (test/fakes/realtime-server.js) answers every input that makes a turn, one after another, a text turn first when it comes before the answer to the user's turn has begun; it used to drop a waiting answer when a second input came. No stored format changes. Round three: the rule runs where the user's turn begins (`LiveKoe.#activity('start')`), for every note gone in and not passed on, and `#said` no longer decides it; the second round's version ran when her turn was written and only for a turn whose words had begun, so a note a sound cut before her first word was lost (or asked for again, and read aloud over a "stop"), as was one her answer to the sound had words for, and, on the phone's speaker, one the provider had finished; those are shown now. annai also shows what it would show at the second strike for what Kokoro meant that is no note's `for_user` (a gated speak's words, a card, a question), for a tap and a sound alike (`#present`, shared with `#backstop`); a switch to another conversation still shows words only. A reminder cut by sound is now a silent card where the release said it aloud (the user's pick, 2026-11-11).
- Why we had to change: on a drive, on the phone's speaker, a bump at the microphone just before her first sound became the user's turn and cut a correction after 58 ms; the provider went on from the middle of its own record of the note 1.1 s later (not "twelve seconds later", as this entry first said: that line is written as her voice ends, and it held 11.3 s), and the user heard the note without its first three words: 1 of 47 notes for the user in three recorded drives. This change's first round asked for the note again as the user's turn ended. On real Gemini Live she then said the whole note twice (8 of 8 runs) and again after "stop" (5 of 6): the ask was a second input, Gemini answers each input with a whole answer of its own, the ask's first, and the ask went in before the transcript of the user's words existed, so no wording could make it one answer or hold it back for a "stop". The unit tests passed because the stand-in dropped the pending answer to the user's turn when the ask came.
- Original design was wrong because: the release treated a note that sound cut as owed aloud, where a tap's cut, the same fact for the note, was already settled by showing it; so it needed an ask at a moment when anything annai sends is answered beside the user's turn. The first round then mended that ask's timing and words, not whether an ask could work there; and the stand-in modelled Gemini answering one input at a time, which no record showed.
- Lesson: on a protocol where each input gets its own answer, add nothing of your own at the user's turn end: settle what was cut with what the program can do alone (show it), by the path its twin already takes, and let the model answer only what the user said. A stand-in answers every input the real provider answers, or a doubled answer passes the tests. A rule about the user's turn belongs where their turn begins, not where her answer to it is written, since the cases that matter are those where she has said nothing yet; and a "said once" assertion means something only at real seconds, as the kit's 10 ms second closes a session before a second answer can start.

## 2026-11-11 Kokoro waits for Koe's answer live, and is told which reminders Koe says
- Changed: Halves' wait for Koe's answer to a typed line (`#koeOwes`, 2026-11-10's wait) holds in live voice too. It ends at Koe's first words (an `out.*` of Koe's), at its `tell_kokoro` (its question is there for Kokoro to read; a live Koe that passes a line on may say nothing first), at its turn ending, at a live session closing or dropping (a session that ends says nothing more), and when no live session can open while Koe stays live (today's spend reached); at most `KOE_FIRST_S` (3.5 s) with a chat Koe, and `SILENT_S` (8 s, live.js's own bound for a session that gives nothing back, now exported) live. GATHER.most still caps every hold. A wake with reminders doesn't wait: while Koe is given them to say (the page in view), Kokoro's wake, which starts at once, carries annai's fact that Koe is saying them now, and not to pass them on. With Koe unset or down, or a live Koe's page out of view (annai shows them at once), there is no such fact; a chat Koe out of view still gets them, and Kokoro the fact.
- Why we had to change: in a recorded drive in live voice, Kokoro's request for a typed line went out before Koe's line was written (a live line is written as her voice ends), so it held no [Koe said] line and the user got two answers; and a reminder's wake woke both halves at once, so Kokoro's first request didn't show Koe saying the reminder, and she said it as well. Koe's own live answers in that drive were written 2.6 to 5.8 s after the user's turn ended (15 of them, median 4.0 s): 3.5 s would have missed 8. A first version held the reminder's wake as well, until Koe's next line: a review found the hold ended at any line of Koe's (one it was saying before the reminder), and that another conversation opened within it left the wake there unanswered, so coming back ran a turn of annai's own that nobody had asked for. With no session able to open, every typed line waited out the whole 8 s.
- Original design was wrong because: the 2026-11-10 wait was made for the one path where the double was measured (a typed line, a chat Koe), with that path's bound; Kokoro's rule ("say it only if Koe hasn't") is read on every path where both halves are given the same thing, and a live Koe's line is written later than a chat Koe's words. For reminders, annai itself gives Koe the words to say: what Kokoro had to wait to read, annai already knew.
- Lesson: a wait for another party's answer covers every input both are given and every path that answer comes by, each bounded by that path's own measured time, and ends at once on every path where no answer can come; but where the program itself hands the other party what it will do, it tells the model that as a fact and nothing waits: a wait holds everything queued behind it, and any line ends it, not only the answer.

## 2026-11-11 Koe passes on what the user said, as they said it
- Changed: Koe's instructions (`KOE_CORE`, `KOE_LIVE` in src/core/koe/instructions.js) and `tell_kokoro`'s description. Passing on is the default: anything the user says that Koe's other lines don't answer (a question, a request, something to do) gets "One moment." and a `tell_kokoro` of what they said, as they said it, not Koe's reading of it, unless it tells Koe to stop (then it stops, and says nothing); Koe asks them nothing of its own, and never says it's done until a note says so (the old "asked for something done" line folded in). "If Kokoro can't be reached, say you can't check that now." is gone from both: annai tells Koe when Kokoro can't answer (§6.8 Failures). The lines for what gets no reply (lyrics, noise, other people, stray words; live, an acknowledgement or thinking aloud) say "no reply: no words, no sound" in place of "Say nothing at all", "Silence is a fine answer" and "say nothing"; and a live Koe's instructions say once that every word it produces is spoken aloud, so it never describes what it is doing or that it is staying quiet.
- Why we had to change: in a recorded drive in live voice, Koe said aloud a phrase naming the quiet it was told to keep, five times; it asked the user back where the request was Kokoro's to answer, said it couldn't check while Kokoro was there, and answered from its own head; and what it did pass on was its reading of the words, so Kokoro answered a question the user hadn't asked.
- Original design was wrong because: the instructions named a state ("silence") for a model whose every output is sound, so the state's name became words; they listed what to do in several cases and left the rest to Koe's judgement, where the safe default was one line away; and a standing excuse line for a rare case was there to reach for whenever Koe was unsure, true or not.
- Lesson: a voice model's instructions say what to do, never a state to be in, since whatever it produces is said; the case left over gets the safe default by name (pass it on, as said); and a line for a rare case that the program already handles is taken out, because a model uses it where it's false.

## 2026-11-11 Only speech cuts her
- Changed: where annai doesn't hear while she speaks (half-duplex: the phone's speaker, or a route annai can't name), a run of sound the microphone holds as her voice begins is the user's turn only if it counts as speech by then, by the rule that starts a turn at every other moment: 250 ms of speech in it (the detector's `minSpeechMs`, the frame her voice begins in included; `LiveInput`, src/core/realtime/input.js). A run that doesn't is dropped as her voice begins, with what was held for it, as everything she plays over is, and nothing of it is sent. Before, any held run with some speech in it became the user's turn there, which cuts her (f99b6cb9, 2026-10-22: "Speech that had begun in the 40 ms before her voice plays … starts the user's turn, which cuts her, rather than being swallowed"); 184c67ab (2026-10-23) had taken one kind out of it, sound over the detector's guessed floor (a car heard as the microphone opens), after it cut her first words in 12 of 15 probes. Her voice also begins again when her stream ran dry mid-answer, and a run held in that gap (her own echo, in a test; and talk the user began in the gap, however long: it is dropped as her voice resumes, as at her end) no longer cuts her there either. Headphones and a car's hands-free, where annai hears while she speaks, are unchanged. The cost, accepted: a word the user begins less than about a quarter second before her voice is talked over, as once she is speaking. In a noisy room she plays at once, so the whole quarter second is exposed; in a quiet one her voice waits while a run is held, so only a word begun in the instant the wait's cap lets her go.
- Why we had to change: drive 3, on the phone's speaker in a car. A bump at the microphone began about 150 ms before her first sound for a note from Kokoro, peaking 28 dB over a floor of -78 dB, with no words in it. The rule took it for the user's turn as her voice began: her voice stopped after 58 ms, and the provider went on from the middle of the note 1.1 s later, so the user heard it without its first words (1 of 47 notes in three drives). The user, asked "Should a sound cut her off only once it has gone on long enough to count as speech (a quarter of a second)?", with that cost stated, picked "Yes, as its own change (Recommended)".
- Original design was wrong because: it took a sound not yet known to be speech as the user's turn. The detector holds a run until 250 ms of speech is in it because shorter sounds are often not speech (a click, a door, a bump); the rule for her voice's first frame answered the same question by a weaker test for one instant, so in that instant everything the test keeps out counted, and the first patch, for one kind of sound, left the next kind through.
- Lesson: a question has one test, and an edge (an instant, a first frame) asks it too, never by a weaker one: an exception that acts before the test has passed lets through what the test keeps out, and patching it one kind at a time finds the next kind on the road. Say what the stricter edge costs, and let the user take it.

## 2026-11-11 A drop is traced
- Changed: the Trace's `live.turn` line, written for each end of the user's turn (`onTurn`, src/core/realtime/input.js; src/core/koe/live.js writes it), gains an `end` value, `dropped`: a run the microphone held as her voice began (or began again, after her stream ran dry) that wasn't 250 ms of speech yet, and was dropped with her voice (2026-11-11 Only speech cuts her). It is no turn: `ms` is 0 (nothing was sent, as for `kept`), `floorDb` and `peakDb` (its loudest frame) as in any line, `minDb`, `keptFrames` and `keptPeakDb` as in any line, and one field only this value has, `speechMs`: how many ms of the run were speech (a turn needs 250). No audio, no words. Every other `live.turn` line is unchanged, and `live.js` ends nothing and learns nothing of the user's level from a `dropped` one. The Trace screen says "a sound dropped as her voice began: 150 ms of speech · floor -78 dB, loudest -51 dB", and the realtime-check timeline (scripts/realtime/analyse.mjs) prints `speech 150 ms`. Measured with a 450 ms synthetic word: begun 60 ms or more before her voice's first frame it has a line; begun 50 ms or less before, it isn't yet a run the detector holds, and has none.
- Why we had to change: before, a run held as her voice began was the user's turn, which cut her and ended as "voice", a line in the Trace; that line is how drive 3's cut was found. When the rule changed so that such a run is dropped, the line went with it, and nothing else wrote one. The cost the user accepted (a word begun just before her voice is talked over) and what it buys (a bump no longer cuts her) could then not be counted in a drive, and the limitation's "revisit when a drive shows a word of the user's lost this way" could only come from the user's memory.
- Original design was wrong because: the Trace's turn line was written only by a turn's end, so a change that made a kind of turn into a drop deleted its record without anyone deciding to. "No turn" had been read as "nothing to say".
- Lesson: when a change turns something the Trace recorded into a drop, the drop gets its own line in the same change, numbers only: an absence in the Trace is evidence only where the thing was asked and answered. A field keeps one meaning (`ms` stays what was sent: 0 for a run that sent nothing); a number only the new kind has is a field of its own, written on that kind alone.

## 2026-11-12 Any answer ends a Stop's hold
- Changed: the Stop's hold (`#stopHeld`, `#stopped.on`; src/core/agent/halves.js) ends in Kokoro's `logged` hook, for a `msg.user` or a `heard` not dismissed, as it already did in Koe's: a line of the user's, whichever half wrote it. So a "Yes." annai's card takes (written through Kokoro's log by `cardTakes`), Allow or Not now tapped on the card (a tap on a pending question of Kokoro's is the same write), and a line Kokoro's own microphone hears while Koe is down all end the hold; a heard line judged not meant for Shiori (dismissed) does not. `Halves.send`'s own clear is gone, since the write covers it: a typed line ends the hold when it is written, not when it is sent. The user's Try again and opening another conversation still end it themselves, since they write no line. The behaviour that changed: a tapped Allow or Not now never ended a Stop's hold before.
- Why we had to change: the final review of the last release found, against the release before it, that after a Stop a spoken "Yes." that annai's card takes no longer ended the hold, so Koe's `tell_kokoro` was refused ("hasn't written or spoken since") after the user had answered. Card-first (2026-11-11 annai's card hears first) moved that write from Koe's log to Kokoro's, and only Koe's log ended the hold; a tapped Allow, written by Kokoro too, had never ended it (in the release before too). The user was asked and picked "Any answer counts": typed, said or tapped, to Shiori or to annai's card.
- Original design was wrong because: "the user has acted since the Stop" had no owner. The hold was cleared as a side effect of four unrelated paths (`send`, Koe's `logged` hook, `tryAgain`, `open`), so a line of the user's that none of them happened to carry skipped it, and each new way to write one (card-first was one) needed someone to remember to add it.
- Lesson: a state that means "the user has acted since X" is cleared in the one place every line of theirs is written (each half's log hook), not on each path that used to carry one; a new way to write their line then ends it unasked.

## 2026-11-12 A held card is written into the conversation left
- Changed: `Agent.log` takes an options object, `{ scope }`: the entry is for that conversation (`#logIn`, src/core/agent/loop.js). Which way it goes is decided in the write lock, as it is written: while that conversation is the one open, as every line of hers is written; otherwise straight into its log, the same record with the same marks, counted by no turn running here, and taken by its transcript once open if it is the one being opened. annai's reminders (`remind`) are written through it too, so their rule, the same as before, is now decided at write time rather than when they are asked for. In live voice, a card or a spoken line Kokoro made while Koe talked that Koe hadn't passed on when the user opens another conversation or starts fresh is written, silently, into the conversation left (above the divider at Start fresh): the same `out.show`, or "Shiori's answer", that annai shows at once when a tap cuts Koe off. A held question is let go, with a quiet `unshown` Trace line there: nobody can answer it in a conversation they left. No stored format changed.
- Why we had to change: at a switch or Start fresh, annai showed only Kokoro's words for the user and the reminders it held; a card Kokoro made never appeared, in the conversation left either, and Kokoro, told "Koe is speaking for you", didn't make it again. `kokoro.log` wrote into whichever conversation was open, and at a switch that is already the other one; only `remind` could name a conversation.
- Original design was wrong because: the write API named no conversation, so "the open one" was implied, and the one writer that needed another (`remind`) carried its own rule, decided when it was called, while the write it made ran later, in the lock, by when the open conversation may have changed.
- Lesson: a write names the record it goes into from its first version, and the rule that picks how (as the open conversation's line or not) runs where the write happens, not when it is asked for: one rule for every writer that needs it.

## 2026-11-12 A question's answer has one reader
- Changed: the `ask` tool's run (src/core/agent/loop.js) takes its answer through `#answer`, as `speak`, `wait` and her question aloud do, instead of keeping its own tapped and typed branches. With voice off, her question shows its buttons; the user taps Shiori (a tap holds voice on and opens the microphone) and says the answer: it is now one heard line for the question, and the model reads "The user said:" with their words after it, where it read "The user wrote: undefined" and the words were never written as a heard line. No stored format changed.
- Why we had to change: found by reading the code and reproduced in a unit test; not seen on a phone. A spoken answer to a question shown with voice off reached an `ask` run that knew only a tap and typing: it fell to the typed branch with no text, told the model "The user wrote: undefined", and wrote a line of the user's with empty text.
