It reads the connection it asks under (the ability's address and list path, the provider's headers, key header and key, as stored) in the saves' queue (`queueSave`, in place of `settled`), so an address typed just before is saved first and no save lands inside the reads, and reads it again when the answer comes: changed, the answer lands nowhere, saying nothing; the same, it is told, by an event (`listed`), to the models part on show for that provider and ability (`data-models`), a part drawn since the tap among them, which draws its rows from the provider's models as read with the answer and keeps the connection the list was asked under. A row's Add reads the connection again first (waiting, greyed, for a save under way); changed, it adds nothing and says "This list is from before the address or key changed. Fetch the model list again." in the toast a failed Add has, its button back (a provider that is gone goes through, and the add says so itself). A row's failed Add draws the part's list as it is by then, not its own. The filter box is made once per part, and an answer redraws only the rows, by the filter's text. Nothing takes a list off the screen when its connection changes: a list on show stays, its Adds refused, until a Fetch lists again (one that fails leaves the old rows) or the page is drawn. models-next had the ability's own address, once saved, take the list away where it stood; its three review rounds found that drop raced the Fetch's check (an old list drawn back under the new address), cleared an Add model by id refusal's words from the one status line, and covered one of the ways a connection changes (not a plugin's sign-in, a key, headers, a list path, a held Save or Remove key, or an Add while an address change waited), so its step back's design check replaced it with the check at the Add. The page still keeps no copy of a list: a draw of the page drops it, and a part drawn after an answer has none.
- Why we had to change: three shapes on main, left by model-list-next's step back: a list fetched under the ability's old address stayed on show after its own address was saved as another, its Add adding a model the new server may not have; a Fetch that answered after the page was drawn again showed nothing, as it drew into the part drawn away; and an answer built the filter box again, so a box the traveller was typing in lost its text, its caret and the keyboard.
- Original design was wrong because: the Fetch's closure held the part it began in, whose list slot was built with its filter box each time: the answer was tied to a view that may be gone, and replaced the control the traveller types in. Nothing tied the answer to what it was asked under, so a change of address could only drop the rows on show, and only where it drew; a drop at one change of the source covers that change only, and races the answer it drops. A copy of the list kept across draws needed dropping at each change of its source (model-list-next took it out).
- Lesson: an answer that comes back later goes to the view on show for what it was asked about, found there by that thing's key (a view drawn since included), never kept for a view drawn later. It carries what it was asked under (an address, a key), and is checked against what is stored where it acts: as it lands, and again at each action it offers (a row's Add), which is refused in words when that changed; it is not taken off the screen at each change of its source. Reads of what it was asked under are made in the queue the writes take, so no write lands inside them. A control a person types in is made once, and an answer redraws only what it owns beside it.

## 2026-11-19 Her reply is her turn, for her voice and her lines' tags

- What changed (voice-reply-next; the Deferred entries "A line's tag doesn't read her reply so far, as the phone's voice does" and "A reply's separate speak calls each start with nothing before them"): `Speaker.begin({ reply })` (`src/core/voice/speaker.js`) takes the reply an utterance is part of, and the speaker hands `browserOptions` the sentences of the reply's earlier utterances before the utterance's own (`before`); a new key is a new reply, and an utterance begun with none (`Voice.say`: a reminder, annai's own card, the live session's line said aloud) starts with nothing before it and leaves the reply under way alone. The loop names her turn's wake at its three begins (`#streamed`, `#say`, `#askAloud`). `tagOf(text, s, byShiori, ui, { before })` takes her reply's sentences so far, and one rule in `languages.js`, `replyLanguage`, gives the voice and the tag the language of a sentence or line that shows none of its own: the first of her earlier sentences that shows one (for Han that says neither Japanese nor Chinese, one written in Han), else null: where nothing in the line or the reply tells, each reader keeps its own default for that Han, the voice the first listed language written in Han, the tag the screens' language when it is one of theirs written in Han (what the page draws with no tag), else the first listed language written in Han, both as before this change. `wording(app)` gives `say(text, { before })`; `src/ui/format.js` exports `replies(store)` (her reply so far for each entry, fed in the log's order: speak's words and her questions under one wake, cut into sentences as the speaker cuts them) and `replyBefore(store, scope, e)` (the same answer, read back from the log for a line drawn alone, page by page up to the first line that ends the reply), which share one test, `turnOf`, for whose reply a line is: a speak, question or card under a wake, not a live session's (one wake for the whole session) and not annai's own. A speak or a question that is none of this turn's (another turn's, a live session's, one with no wake) ends the reply under way in both, and replyBefore answers at once, with no read, for a line that is no reply's; annai's own card (said by `Voice.say`, so for her voice neither part of her reply nor its end) is skipped by both. The Conversation, the dock's bubble, Driving's card, Replay's card and a trip card's last line (drawn with her wording, so the language she answers in applies, as in the Conversation) give her lines their reply, and `follow` (the Conversation's live path) shows each event after the one before it, so a line that waits on a stored text is not passed by one that comes after it. Replay's card entries (`readJourney`) carry their entry's `seq` and `wake`.
- Why we had to change: with Chinese and Japanese both listed, a short line of Han that says neither (「好的。」, 「東京駅。」) was read in her reply's language by the phone's voice only within one speak call, and by its tag only within one bubble: her second speak call's 「東京駅。」 after 「今日は新宿に行きましょう。」 was read with the Chinese voice under [en, zh, ja], and 「好的。」 drawn as its own bubble after 「我们明天去故宫吧。」 was tagged Japanese under [en, ja, zh] while the voice read it Chinese. (With no reply before it, the tag's screens-first default and the voice's order differ, as they did; the first build of this branch made both the order's, and its final review found that no line of her real sentences reaches that case, so the change would have moved the font of the traveller's own typed lines and trip names for nothing measured.)
- Original design was wrong because: one question, "what language is this sentence of hers in?", was answered by two readers over two different units, each the one it happened to hold (the speaker's utterance, the screen's bubble), neither of them the reply; and each kept its own last rule. The unit that is the reply, her turn, was already on every line she writes (`wake`), and in the loop's hand at every utterance it begins.
- Lesson: a rule that reads context takes the same unit at every reader, cut where every reader can tell it from what it already holds (an id on each record, here the turn's), never the unit each reader happens to have; a reader that draws one record alone reads that context from the log by the same rule, not from a memory of its own; and where nothing in the context tells, each reader keeps the default it had, unless a measurement shows one better for both: no line of her real sentences reached the shared last rule that this branch first built (the "tie" on them was no evidence), and it changed the font of every Han-only line with nothing before it where the list's order and the screens' language disagree.

## 2026-11-19 A read-change-write reads what its write stores
- Changed: `Workspace.update(scope, path, fn, opts)` read every file as JSON. It now reads a file by the type its write will store (`opts.type`, else the path's extension, the rule `write` already used): a JSON file as its value, any other as its text, so `fn` gets the profile's Markdown as a string. Settings → Shiori's name field (`saveCallMe` in `scopes.js`) is the first caller to change a text file this way. One case moved: a path with no extension, where a JSON value was written as an object (stored as JSON text under the octet-stream type), used to give `fn` the parsed value and now gives the text; such a caller passes `opts.type: 'application/json'`. No caller of `update` has such a path (the others are `.json` files).
- Why we had to change: the field changes one line of `/me/profile.md` while Shiori may write the same file at that moment. A read before the lock and a write after it would drop her write; `update` was the one call that held the path's lock across the read and the write, and it could only do so for JSON. The first version of the field read and wrote in two steps outside it.
- Original design was wrong because: the helper's read took "a file is JSON" for the one kind its first callers had, while its write already took the kind by `opts.type ?? typeForPath(path)` and stored a string as it is. Two ends of one operation used two rules for what a file is, so the first text file did not work (a JSON parse of prose threw), and the one place where the rules differ at the margin (an extension-less JSON file) was never stated.
- Lesson: a read-change-write helper takes a file's kind by the same rule at its read and its write, from its first version (one function for the kind, used by both). A helper that reads only the kind its first callers had breaks on the next kind, and where the rule leaves a case to a caller (JSON with no extension), the docs say which option makes it right.

## 2026-11-19 A refused write is a full device by its name and side, not the quota's name alone

- What changed (storage-next; the storage-full design, option 1; its fix rounds): `Store.tx` (`src/core/types.js`, `src/core/store-idb.js`, `storeError` in `src/core/errors.js`) rejects with `StorageFullError` for more than `QuotaExceededError`: in a read-write transaction, also for an `UnknownError` (from a request, the transaction, or fn), and for an abort the browser makes after every request in it succeeded (no request error came up to the transaction; runTx marks that error in a WeakSet). A connection that can't be opened counts as a write in either mode, for the first open (`createIdbStore`) and for any connect inside `tx` (a transaction that starts with no connection, or the reopen of the one retry): by `UnknownError`, or by `AbortError` ("The connection was closed", which Chromium gives for the reopen of a connection its failed write had closed); both were outside the rule before. When fn rejects with a request's `AbortError`, runTx waits for the transaction's own end and rejects with its error if it has one (the transaction's `UnknownError`, not its request's `AbortError`). A read-only transaction keeps its error unless it is the open that failed; in either mode an error of annai's own thrown in `fn` (a name outside the browser's) is rejected as it is, while an `UnknownError` thrown in a read-write `fn` is converted like a request's. The memory store (tests) applies the same names. `StorageFullError`'s message and `shown` became "There's no room left. Free up space on this device, then try again.", true for a write, a removal and a read alike, with no claim that holds for some devices only (a full iPhone refuses even a removal); the card (`src/ui/storage-full.js`) leads with the phone's own Settings, by device (`deviceName`: iPhone, Android phone, any other), says first what the caller did not do ("Nothing was removed.") or that a typed line is back in the box, suggests removing a trip (and offers Go to Trips) only where a delete works on a full disk (not on an iPhone or iPad, nor after a removal was refused), and replaces an earlier storage card only when it says the same.
- Why we had to change: a full phone never raises the origin quota's error. The builder's probe of 2026-11-19 on Linux builds of WebKit 2359 and Chromium 1243 (the Playwright 1.63.0 container, a tmpfs profile filled by annai's own 1 MiB writes; its output wasn't kept; not yet on a phone): WebKit answers every request of a write and aborts its commit with no error at all (annai threw "AbortError: The transaction was aborted." after its retry); with several puts in one transaction its second fails with UnknownError "Unable to store record in object store", the rest, in flight, with AbortError, and the transaction ends with an UnknownError of its own. Chromium aborts a write of large values with "DataError: Failed to write blobs (IOError)"; with no byte free at all, a removal's transaction fails its request with AbortError and the transaction with "UnknownError: Connection is closing because of: IO error", the next read fails "InvalidStateError: Backend aborted error", and the reopen fails "AbortError: The connection was closed" (the first round's note says the reopen gave "UnknownError: Internal error opening backing store"; the second probe saw the AbortError there, and a new connection opened at that point fail with an UnknownError). A WebKit first open at the brim failed too (its error wasn't recorded). The traveller read "The transaction was aborted." or "The connection was closed." and was never shown the storage card.
- Original design was wrong because: it took the one refusal the platforms document (the quota's) for the only one, without filling a disk to see; and the open, first or again, sat outside the try that decides what a failed operation rejects with, so its error skipped the rule.
- Lesson: a rule about what a platform refuses is learned from the platform refusing, on the real engines, every shape it gives, and again after each change that touches it; decided by the error's name and by which side ended the operation (annai's code finished and the browser still refused), never by its words; and every step of the operation, the first open and a retry's reconnect included, passes the one door that decides it. Where the platform gives no reason (WebKit's abort), the rule accepts that another failure of the same shape reads the same.

## 2026-11-19 A typed line a failed write didn't keep goes back to the box

- What changed (storage-next; its fix rounds 1 and 2): `Agent.send(text, { typed })` (and `Halves.send`, which passes it through) marks a line typed in the type box; the dock and the box's hand-over send it so, a hand-over to another conversation too (`wake({ typed })`). When no write kept a typed line, `#unsaved` hands the words back, never holds them (one case was first accepted, a line typed while its conversation was opening that stayed queued behind a refused reminder, because `remind` failed the open before `#logWakes` could hand it back; it went with "2026-11-20 A reminder is shown before its entry is written, and a write nobody waits on says so"): it emits `unsaved` with the words (joined by newlines) and the error (Halves passes its halves' on), takes the line off the queue if it is still on it, and marks it, so a line comes back once however many paths fail on it. The paths are a write of the line (a turn's start, a gathering turn the user stopped, a queued line written as another conversation opens or with nothing open, the typed answer to her question or to annai's card, a spoken no to the card written first) and the turn itself: `#drain`'s catch gives back the typed lines the failed turn took and those that waited behind it (a phone that refuses even a read fails the turn before its first write, and a line typed while she worked is on the queue, where nothing drains it again). Live Koe's own write (`koe/live.js`) carries no typed mark: a refusal there hands the line to Kokoro, whose turn gives it back (Halves' failure route). The dock puts the words in its one type box, whichever conversation is open, before anything typed there since (the box takes the focus only on the conversation's own screen), and its hand-over does the same when it fails before it passed the words on (`notSent`: the read of a file kept earlier). The storage card says the message is back in the box: first for a typed line; after "Your files didn't reach Shiori." only when the hand-over failed before it passed the words on (the dock's `notSent`). A refusal gets one card (`explainStorageFull` shows an error's card once, so the turn that stopped on the same error adds none), and a card replaces only an earlier one that says the same, so one that says something else (a file wasn't kept) stays. The dock clears its box as the message is taken on the file path too, so words given back while files are kept stay. The notices that carried storage words under another title (Shiori stopped, a photo not sent) carry their error, and main.js shows the storage card in their place, with what they say first.
- Why we had to change: the dock empties the box as it sends, and the loop takes a line off its queue before writing it, so a write that failed (a full phone: WebKit's first write of the turn fails; Chromium at no byte free refuses even a read) lost the traveller's words; "Shiori stopped: Something went wrong" was all that was left. The user's pick of 2026-11-14 (timeline design, line 204) was for a line typed when no conversation can be made (storage full, a store failing): "Put it back in the type box (Recommended)". This change extends it, as the main session's call, to a write failing inside an open conversation. Round 1 decided box or card by the conversation the words were for (the event carried it; the dock put them in the box only when it was the open one, else on the card as "Your message wasn't saved: …"). The design check of 2026-11-19 found that choice the state behind the findings that kept coming: the dock read the target when the event came, which an open under way had already moved (the words went to the card and the box stayed empty); a card holding the words was taken down by any later storage card; and the words lived in memory in more places than the loop could cover path by path (the dock's hand-over, Halves' route closure, the queue, a turn's taken wakes, a pending answer's `got`). Two more ways to lose a line showed at the turn's boundary: a line typed while she worked stayed on the queue when her turn was what the phone stopped (a retype was written twice, a reload lost it), and a read that failed before the turn's first write took the line with it. On a real engine (the builder's probe, Chromium at no byte free) the line sent on a full phone was gone: the box empty, no card about it.
- Original design was wrong because: the words left the only place that held them (the box) at the send, before anything had kept them, and no path back existed for the one step that could fail after; then the way back was built path by path, so each path decided on its own whether to hand back, hold, or show a card; and round 1 added a second decision, by conversation, that needed a state (the target at the event, a card that holds words) to make, for a box that is the app's and not a conversation's.
- Lesson: a person's words leave the place they were typed before the write that keeps them, so the writer that fails to keep them hands them back, marked as typed by the place that sent them (a line from elsewhere has no box to go back to): one door for every path that writes them, and one at the turn's boundary for what a failed turn leaves, taken or waiting; a line given back is marked so it comes back once; one rule for a refused line (always handed back, never kept queued in memory, where a reload loses it); and the place that receives them is the one box, never chosen by a state read at the moment of the event (the open conversation, a card that holds the words). What no path can hand back (a spoken line, a tap, a kept file) is accepted as a limitation at design time, not met with a path of its own.

## 2026-11-19 Wikipedia in the traveller's languages: a part's file for each edition, merged where it is read

- What changed (places-lang-next; the places-lang design's option A, with its spec check's B1): `readingOrder(app, locale)` (`src/core/languages.js`) is the language Shiori always answers in if one is fixed, the languages listed in their order (the phone's, with none chosen), then English. `src/core/wiki.js` has each language's edition (`editionsOf`, by host; a phone language annai has no row for has none), one edition's nearest articles (`nearUrl`, `articlesOf`; `wikiSaid` for an edition's `{"error": …}` answer, which is that edition not answering), their merge (`merged`: each article once, by its Wikidata id, under the first edition of the order) and an article's address with its title as written (`articleUrl`: only `%`, `?`, `#`, `\` and the brackets encoded, as chat's links end at a bracket). nearby asks every edition at once within one ASK_MS, names an edition that didn't answer (with the offline pack's articles of it where the pack reaches), and takes Wikipedia's nearest 30 at most, online and from the pack (`WIKI_ROWS`, what English alone was asked for), so more editions bring no more than that (the 40 rows are the nearest of both sources, so up to 30 of them can be articles); geocode, reverse and the floor send `accept-language` (`nominatimLanguages`, `src/core/nominatim.js`); the floor is `knowledgeFloor(orderOf)`, built in the web plugin's `activate` where the settings can be read (it replaces the constant `KNOWLEDGE`): each edition of the order runs the floor's own tries, all editions at once, the whole floor within one `FLOOR_MS` (9 s, a second inside the chain's 10 s reserve), and the rows are the first edition's in the order that found any of what answered, no rows and a failure being the failure, named by the part that failed; the floor never rests in the chain (asked last, within its own time, one service of it failing says nothing of the rest), and the chain says "Nothing found" only when no provider failed. The pack's stored format: a Wikipedia part is one file for each edition, English's at `/offline/wikipedia/<id>.json` where it was, another's at `<id>.<edition>.json`; a row is `[title, lat, lng, summary, wikidata]`, `null` for a page with no Wikidata id; a Wikipedia file's manifest entry carries `edition`, a kept file the manifest doesn't name taking it from the edition its path was wanted as (English's stays unknown, so it is downloaded again once another edition is listed). `format` stays 1. `wanted(plan, editions)` names the files a plan's pack holds, downloaded edition by edition in the order, and `lacksIds` reads an English entry without `edition` (written before editions, its rows without ids) as missing once another edition is listed; it stays as it is until it is written again at its path. A source (OpenStreetMap, an Overpass server, an edition, Wikivoyage) that doesn't answer or refuses three parts in a row is left for the rest of the run, the plan's parts left and the guides left counted among those that didn't download; an answer annai can't use fails its part only, one cut off in its body counting as no answer. `downloadPack` takes `editions`, `PackKeeper` takes `locale` and wakes on a change of the editions the app settings give, `Pack.pages(p, radius, editions)` and `Pack.name(p, editions)` merge as they read and read only the listed editions, and `packNamer` gives the recap and Replay their namer. `short` counts a Chinese, Japanese or Korean character as 2 of its 200 and cuts at the last space or after the last mark, whichever is later. Wikipedia is credited as `*.wikipedia.org`, and the credits test reads a host the code makes from a value as its whole domain.
- Why we had to change: the user's pick of 2026-11-14 was "Read the traveller's language first, English when that has no article", and annai read English Wikipedia only and left Nominatim's language to the browser's own header.
- Original design was wrong because: a pack's Wikipedia part was one file named by its place alone, so nothing in its name or its record said which language it held; a second language could come only under a new name or with a stored "which languages" to compare. The design's first answer, merging the editions where they are fetched, would have put the order into every file: a reorder (one tap, and the first language is dictation's) downloading every part again, an article twice where parts overlap (清水寺 in one part's Japanese, Kiyomizu-dera only in the next one's English), and English titles fetched through `langlinks` for the guides.
- Lessons set aside, and held: "Bump a cache's format version with every change, and never teach the reader old caches" is set aside in both clauses: a pack may be all a traveller has offline, `readManifest` gives null for another version, and the keeper refreshes only a pack it has, so a bump would empty every pack at the update; the reader takes older shapes field by field instead ("Read older releases' files as written": a row of four is English without an id, an entry without `edition` English from before editions). "When a record stands for files, it names them, and every reader and copy takes what it names" holds for the pack's readers and the downloader (the manifest names each file with its part's id and its edition, the reader takes the edition from the entry, and a file the manifest doesn't name takes it from the path it was wanted at, never from what the directory holds); the archive copies the trip's whole scope, `/offline/` with it, so a pack imported onto a phone with other languages is completed there. "Keep 'unknown' apart from 'empty'": a page without a Wikidata id is `null`, its edition's own, once by title, and an entry with no `edition` key means "written before editions", which `lacksIds` tests by the key. "Before a replace, rename or removal, list everything keyed by the old name": `fresh`, `keepOld` and `gone` in downloadPack, the keeper's `status` and `estimate`, `counted` (an article once by its id), `namesFor` (English's titles only, so the guides are found as before), `loadPack` (the manifest's paths), and the test fakes; the update path is tested with a pack main's own code wrote. An older annai given a new pack reads each Wikipedia entry as English, so a Japanese title is linked on en.wikipedia.org and an article in two editions shows twice; its `status` finds English's paths and asks for nothing; its next download deletes the other editions' files, which its plan doesn't name, and the new annai downloads them again: two tabs on either side of an update take turns.
- Lesson: a cache of parts that come in several variants keeps one file for each part and variant, named by both, with the variant said in the record that names the file: a variant added or dropped is files added or dropped at the next download (the reader takes only the listed variants at once), a reorder stores nothing, and a variant that failed is no new file (its old copy stays, or there is none), never a file claiming what it lacks. Variants are merged where they are read, by an identity the source gives (a Wikidata id), not where they are fetched. A cache that may be someone's only copy keeps its format version, and its reader takes older shapes field by field.

## 2026-11-19 A switch a protocol offers shows where the provider's description names it
- Changed: a choice (`choices`, G3) whose tunable the endpoint type's own tunables have `false` now shows on a provider's page only where that provider's description names the tunable for the ability (provider.js, `named`), or where it is already on (typed in the JSON: there it must be able to be turned off). The OpenAI-compatible voice (speech-openai 0.7.0, API 2) gained `readSpeechStream`, a `stream` tunable (`false`), a Stream switch among its choices, and `pcmOnly` (the ids' prefixes of voices that answer only raw audio); OpenRouter's description (2.6.0) names `stream: false` on its voice ability, `stream: false` on Grok, Voxtral and Kokoro, and `pcmOnly: ["heygen/", "google/"]`. Raw audio is played at the rate its answer's Content-Type names, read by a strict rule; an answer with no rate, or one outside it, fails that voice.
- Why we had to change: streamed voice for providers other than Gemini (the user's ask, 2026-10-19) came, by the voice probe of 2026-11-19, to Fish Audio's voice on OpenRouter, the only OpenRouter voice that starts 2 seconds or more sooner, off by default (chief's decision C135). The switch had to live on the OpenAI-compatible protocol, which OpenAI's, the local servers' and any custom address's voices speak too; their raw audio's rate was never measured, so on their pages the same switch could have made a voice fail wherever its answer names no rate or isn't raw audio.
- Original design was wrong because: `choices` assumed that what a protocol offers, every provider speaking it can do. That held while Gemini's protocol served Google alone; a protocol many servers speak differs per server, and annai keeps those differences in tunables the descriptions set ("their differences are tunables", speech-openai), so the offer has to follow the description too.
- Lesson: a protocol type many servers speak offers on every page what only some of them can do; a choice that is off by default shows only where the provider's description names its tunable, or where it is already on (so it can be turned off). A capability that varies within one provider is the description's per model, and what an answer must say to be played (its rate) is read from the answer by our own rule, never assumed for a server that wasn't measured.

## 2026-11-19 edit reads text copied from the now block's layout
- Changed: Shiori's `edit` tool (the files plugin) takes `old` as given first; when that matches nothing and it holds `<lt>`, it reads `<lt>` as "<", in `new` too, and edits when so read it matches exactly once. Her to-do list (`/trip/todo.md`) is shown in the now block with its "<" written `<lt>`, so a line she copies from there to change matches.
- Why we had to change: the to-do list is shown whole in every request's now block, and a "<" in it must not end the block (`</now>`), so it is written as the TSV views write it, `<lt>`, a form her instructions already explain, and the list's label says so. She changes the list with `edit`, from what she sees there.
- Original design was wrong because: `edit` matched only the file's bytes, while two of her views write "<" as `<lt>` (search's TSV rows, and now the list), so text with a "<" copied from a view matched nothing and the edit failed.
- Lesson: a tool that edits text its caller copied from a view also accepts it in the view's layout: the text as given first; the view's `<lt>` escape undone only when that matches nothing and the undone form matches exactly once, and the new text read in the same layout.

## 2026-11-19 A summary's range is what it stands for
- Changed: a `compaction` record's `data.from` (a summary's, mode warm or cold) is the first event the summary stands for: the `from` of the summary it was written from, just after a fresh start's `to` when it follows one, else the conversation's first event in context. It was the transcript's `from`, which after a compaction is that compaction's own event. Old records are read as written; the header ("events a–b") and the Trace's line read the field as before.
- Why we had to change: a second summary is written from the first summary and what follows it, so it stands for everything from the start, but its header named only the part from the first compaction's own event on, so `recall` given that range missed the earliest part. With a cold return over 100,000 tokens summarized, a long conversation gets several summaries.
- Original design was wrong because: the field was filled from the reader's position (`transcript.from`, the first event the transcript holds, which a compaction sets to its own event), not from what the new record covers; the two agree only before the first compaction.
- Lesson: a record that says what range it covers computes it from what it stands for (the record it was made from, or the mark that started it), never from a reader's position that a record of the same kind moves.

## 2026-11-20 A Wikipedia address ends in a character chat's links keep
- Changed: chat's renderer (`src/ui/markdown.js`) ends a bare address at any mark of one fixed set, `ADDRESS_ENDS` (`src/core/address-ends.js`: the Japanese and Chinese stops, commas, full-width brackets and closing quotes, `。、，；！？（）」』”`, each with its reason beside it), wherever the mark stands, and at a `[`, `]`, `{`, `}` or `|` (MediaWiki refuses them in a title; an IP-literal address such as `http://[::1]:8080/x` no longer links in chat); it keeps a pair of ASCII parentheses that closes inside the address, and in a `[text](address)` destination (one level of it, GFM's rule); a `_` opens or closes italics only at the edge of a word of Latin letters or digits (CommonMark's rule, for those: `_重要_` in Japanese or Chinese text is italics as it was); `[address](address)` keeps the address as its text; the text of a `[text](address)` takes no `[`. `pageUrl` (`src/core/wiki.js`), which writes every Wikipedia and Wikivoyage address annai hands Shiori (nearby's rows online and from the offline pack, `guide`, the search floor's guide and articles), reads the same set: it encodes any of its marks anywhere in a title as UTF-8 bytes (`…/モーニング娘%E3%80%82`, `…/藤岡弘%E3%80%81`), and a title's last `.,;:!'"` as the renderer leaves it out of a bare address (`…/Washington,_D.C%2E`, `…/Yahoo%21`; `?` was encoded wherever it is). Wikipedia opens a title so encoded (plain GETs, 2026-11-20: `%2E`, `%21`, `%E3%80%82`, `%E3%80%81`, `%EF%BC%88` and `%EF%BC%89` in a zh title). An address whose title holds none of these is as it was, byte for byte; the pack keeps titles, not addresses, so nothing stored changes.
- Why we had to change: a bare address in a card's text was ended by a bracket or whitespace and lost a last ASCII stop, so that a sentence's own full stop isn't taken into the link; an article whose title ends in a stop was linked without it, to another page or to none. And Japanese and Chinese text has no space after a stop, so `…/清水寺。次は八坂神社` was one link, as was `（…/清水寺）` and a list `…/清水寺、金閣寺`; the first version of this change, which encoded only a title's last ASCII character, left all of that (the final review found it). The `_` rule: a lone `_` earlier in a reply paired with the `_` inside an address as italics, so `notes_today and …/Big_Sur here` linked `…/Big`, another page (the final review's F5); the word's edge is Latin letters and digits only, since a closing `_` before Japanese (`_…/清水寺_です`) must stay out of the address. `[address](address)`: it drew an empty link, the inner address filtered out together with its text (the final review's F4). No `[` in a link's text: a run of them was rescanned to the end of the line from each one (660 ms for one 20,000-character line).
- Original design was wrong because: places-lang-next encoded the brackets for the renderer's rule of where a bare address ends, but not for its other rule, what it leaves out at the end; and the renderer's rule was a guess about its text (a stop comes last, before a space), true of English and not of the traveller's languages, so a fix at the writer alone was one more case where the reader kept guessing.
- Lesson: a value written for a reader with rules of its own is encoded for all of them, at the one function that writes it. The marks both sides must agree on come from one fixed set that the reader and the writer both read (`ADDRESS_ENDS`); two lists remain that agree only because the tests keep them from drifting: the ASCII brackets (the renderer's patterns and `pageUrl`'s `[%?#\\()]`) and the last `.,;:!?'"` (the renderer's, `?` apart, and `pageUrl`'s `.,;:!'"`). The tests go through that reader with every member of the set at the start, middle and end of the value, every printable ASCII character at its end, and the marks a title may hold outside the set (`【推しの子】`, `京・`) at its end, never only the cases a review found. What the reader can only guess (a title's own last stop against a sentence's, a raw title the writer didn't encode, text straight after an address with no space) stays a strict rule and a proposed known limitation (K1), not yet approved, not a wider guess.

## 2026-11-20 The offline pack's limits go: no byte cap, no cap on its parts or guides
- Changed (editions-a-next): the pack's 20 MB (`MAX_BYTES`) is gone with every part that served it: `save` never refuses, the manifest's `full` is neither written nor read (an older annai's `full: true` is ignored, so what such a pack lacks reads as missing and "Download the rest" brings it), the download's rank sort is gone (the parts come in the plan's order, each Wikipedia part in every edition in turn), the keeper's "a full pack is complete" rule, the panel's limit line, `downloadPack`'s and `PackKeeper`'s `maxBytes`, and `full` in the `pack.download` event (the Trace still reads it on old events). The plan's caps went too: `MAX_OSM` and `MAX_WIKI` (100 parts each), `MAX_GUIDES` (12), `PackPlan.cut`, `PackStatus.cut` and the panel's "longer than a pack holds" line. `SIZES` comes from the editions design's measurements, each with its reason (`guideEdition` new), and `estimate(files, guides, editions)` counts a guide in each Wikivoyage edition asked.
- Why we had to change: the user, 2026-11-19: "why 20mb limit?", then "I doubt if we need a limit.. if we have one, maybe make it also user changeable?". The 20 MB came from the first plan of the pack (2026-10-16) with no measurement; three made-up trips measured 3 to 12 MB; the part caps had no written reason either, and cut a 21-day road trip of 46 stops by 34 stops and legs.
- Original design was wrong because: a limit chosen without a measurement grew parts that existed only for it: an order of the download (the first language at every stop before the second anywhere), a field in the stored manifest, a rule in the keeper's status (a full pack has nothing missing), a line and its translations on the panel, an option on two APIs, and two rounds of tests about refreshing at the limit. A second cap, on the parts, was set alongside it; once the bytes no longer bound anything, it was the cap that cut ordinary trips.
- Lesson: a limit set without a measurement grows parts that exist only for it (an order, a stored flag, a status rule, words on a screen); when it goes, list each and remove it, and read an old record's flag as saying nothing rather than migrating it. A second cap on the same thing becomes the bound once the first one binds nothing: give each its own reason or none.

## 2026-11-20 Wikivoyage in English and the traveller's editions: a guide's file for each edition, named by English's title, and the editions asked kept apart
- Changed (editions-a-next): Wikivoyage is read in English and in each edition of the reading order that has one (`guideEditions` and the literal `VOYAGE` set of 24 hosts in `src/core/wiki.js`; a language without one is never asked). The `guide` tool's answer is a part for each edition in the reading order, each its title, "(<language> Wikivoyage)", its address, its introduction and its own section names; English is asked first by the name with its text and its `langlinks`, the others by the titles it links to, and with no English page the next edition by the name (`converttitles`) links the rest; an edition that doesn't answer is named in one line; a `section` is matched in every part. The pack's stored format: a guide's file in another edition is `/offline/wikivoyage/g<hash of the English title>.<edition>.json` beside English's (`{ title, text }`, as English's), each other edition's titles from one `langlinks` request on English Wikivoyage for every 50 guides; English turns a name into a place once, and the pack stores one kind of guide, English's page and its links: a name English has no page for (a disambiguation page is its answer, a list, not a missing page: no edition is asked it) is asked of the editions in the reading order in turn, only while a name is left (`prop=pageprops|langlinks&lllang=en`, 50 a request, `redirects`, `converttitles`, disambiguation pages passing the name on), the first edition with a page by it deciding, and that page's link to English is the guide, found again on English (redirects followed, a disambiguation page left out) and named by the names that led to it; a page with no link to English is no guide, so a guide only the traveller's edition has is not in the pack (chief's decision C154); a guide's manifest entry gains `also: [{ edition, title }]` (each edition English's page links to, whether or not its text came; the file is `guideFile(title, edition)`, never stored), written on every new entry; the manifest gains `voyage`, the editions the last guides pass that ran to its end asked (English first). `format` stays 1. An edition whose lookup fails stays out of `voyage` with a failure, and the guides keep what the pack had in it; one whose names lookup fails ends the turn of editions asked by name (it and the ones after it stay out of `voyage`), and the guides the pack had by the names left stay; English's text is fetched first, and a guide is in the pack by its English file. `Pack.guide(name, editions)` (one caller, the guide tool) finds one guide, by its English title, a name that led to it, its title in an edition, or its title without its bracket (only when one guide has it), and returns each of the editions given that it is in as `{ edition, title, text }` in the order given, the text empty where the pack has no readable file ([] for none; it was one `{ title, text }` or null), so the tool reads online an edition the guide links to whose text didn't come. The keeper reads a pack whose `voyage` lacks one of the traveller's guide editions as missing, and its estimate counts those editions' guides (`SIZES.guideEdition`); English's guides still fresh are not fetched again. The knowledge floor's guide follows English's top hit's links instead of searching each edition. Wikivoyage is credited as `*.wikivoyage.org`.
- Why we had to change: the user, 2026-11-19: "can we pull the english and their edition? because politics orz. want to avoid potential conflicts caused by language based biases orz like some of the geopolitical sensitive issues orz". annai read English Wikivoyage alone.
- Original design was wrong because: a guide's record named one file and one title, and nothing recorded which editions had been looked in, so a missing edition file could mean "that edition has no such guide" as well as "never asked" (a pack from before, or a language added since): a status that reads files alone can't tell "nothing more to come" from "not yet asked".
- Lessons set aside, and held: "Bump a cache's format version with every change" is set aside as on 2026-11-19 (a pack may be someone's only offline copy): the reader takes the older shape field by field (no `also` is English alone, no `voyage` is English alone, `full` is ignored). "To tell new records from old, write the field on every new record and test for the key": `also` is written on every new guide entry (`[]` for none) and `voyage` on every new manifest. An older annai reads a new pack's guides in English, its status asks for nothing it didn't before, and its next download deletes the other editions' files (its plan doesn't name them); the newer one reads that manifest's `voyage` as English alone and fetches them again.
- Lesson: when variants of a cached thing are found through one variant (another edition's titles through English's links), name every variant's file by that variant's identity plus the variant, so a rename elsewhere moves nothing; and record which variants the last complete pass asked, apart from which files exist: an absent file means "none there" only for a variant that was asked, and a status compares the variants wanted with those asked, not with the files.

## 2026-11-20 Wikimedia's 3 requests at once, through one gate
- Changed (editions-a-next): `wikimediaTurn(url, job, signal)` in `src/core/wiki.js`: every request annai sends to a Wikipedia or Wikivoyage host (the places tools, the knowledge floor and the offline pack, each at its own door: places' `ask`, the floor's `json`, the pack's `ask`) waits for one of `WIKIMEDIA_AT_ONCE` (3) turns for the whole app, in the order asked; any other host's request runs at once. A turn ends with its job or its signal, whichever is first, and an ended turn passes straight to the next waiting. The floor asks Wikipedia's editions first, in the reading order, then the guide (one request for English's best match with its links), and `FLOOR_MS`'s reason says what fits at 2.5 s an answer under the gate. The pack's Wikipedia and Wikivoyage requests have the foreground's 25 s (`WIKIMEDIA_MS`), Overpass's its two minutes, so a hung request holds a turn for 25 s at most.
- Why we had to change: Wikimedia's published rule (mediawiki.org, "Wikimedia APIs/Rate limits", read 2026-11-20): "Limit the number of concurrent requests to 3 or fewer", counted across all its sites. The floor sent 4 at once with three languages, and a guide in several editions adds more; the pack downloads beside the tools.
- Original design was wrong because: each tool decided "at once" for its own call ("one request a host: the etiquette's 'in series' is for bulk clients"), while the rule counts the client; calls that run side by side (nearby, the floor, a pack's download) can't see each other's requests.
- Lesson: a provider's limit on requests at once counts the client, not the call: one gate that every caller's request to that provider goes through, at each caller's one door, keyed by the provider's hosts; a freed turn is handed straight to the next waiter (a waiter only woken to take it can be overtaken by a request asked in between), and a turn ends with its request's own end signal too, so a caller whose request never settles holds no turn.

## 2026-11-20 nearby: an article once with every edition that has it; places and articles each cut to its own count, and said when cut
- Changed (editions-b-next): nearby's answer lists up to 40 places (`ROWS`) and up to 50 Wikipedia articles (`ARTICLES`, `src/core/wiki.js`), each list cut to its own count, nearest first, then sorted together (it was the nearest 40 of both, articles 30 at most, so either kind could crowd the other out); each edition is asked for 50 (was 30). An article's row is named by the title in the first edition of the reading order that has it (its own list's title, else Wikidata's), and its note holds its address in each edition that has it, in the order, joined by " · " (was the first edition's address alone): with more than one edition, one Wikidata request after the lists, inside their limit (`wbgetentities`, `props=sitelinks`, `sitefilter` of the editions' sites, the kept articles' ids: `sitelinksUrl`, `titlesOf`, `siteOf`). New endings: "These are the nearest 40 places (out to M m) and the nearest 50 Wikipedia articles (out to M m); farther ones within R m aren't listed." when a list held more than its count or an edition's answer was full; "Wikidata didn't answer, so a row may leave out an edition that has its article."; from the offline pack, "The offline pack holds each edition's nearest 20 Wikipedia articles at each stop and every 15 km of the way, so Wikipedia has more." The pack's rows put each held edition's cut summary and address in the note, joined the same way. `merged` returns each article with `also`, its rows in the later editions. The description gains one sentence: an article is one row with its address in each of the user's editions that has it, and editions can tell a contested place's story differently. The knowledge floor's Wikipedia rows come from every edition that found any, taken in turn in the order, 8 in all (they were the first edition's that found any). Wikidata's host takes Wikimedia's turns (`isWikimedia`).
- Why we had to change: the user, 2026-11-19: "can we pull the english and their edition? because politics orz. want to avoid potential conflicts caused by language based biases orz like some of the geopolitical sensitive issues orz"; for Wikipedia "both. why do we have a 30 article limit?", then "Measure, then set (Rec.)".
- Original design was wrong because: an edition's list names an article only among that edition's own nearest, so "every edition that has it" could show only where each list happened to hold it (the nearest 50 at Tokyo Station: 10 rows with two or more editions by the lists, 27 by Wikidata; Edo, Marunouchi and the 1945 air raid missing from Japanese's own list); the 30 and the shared 40 had no measurement behind them, and one shared count let a long list of one kind leave the other none; the answer never said it was cut.
- Lesson: when one item is listed by several variants of a source, which variants have it comes from the item's own record (one request for the items kept), never from which variants' lists happened to name it; and two kinds of rows in one answer each get their own count, with its own reason and its own cut line, before they are merged in the order the reader needs.

## 2026-11-20 The pack's Wikipedia second stage: a file of its own kind for each part and set of editions
- Changed (editions-b-next): with more than one edition read, `wanted()` gives each Wikipedia part one more `Want` after its lists (`edition: null`, `editions`: the editions read, sorted): its file is `morePath(part, editions)`, `/offline/wikipedia/<id>.x.<editions sorted, joined by ".">.json`, of manifest kind `wikipedia-editions` (`PackFile.kind` gains it; `readManifest` takes it), holding `{ at, pages }`, each row `[title, lat, lng, summary, id, edition]`: each edition's summary of the articles the part's lists hold that its own list lacks, found through Wikidata (50 ids a request) and then by title (`summariesUrl`, 20 a request), at the place the lists give the item. It runs only when every edition's list of its part is there (else it is left for the next run, counted among the parts that didn't download with that list's failure, so the keeper's rule holds unchanged); any request that fails fails it, and no file is written (an old copy stays); it is kept while its lists are, and fetched again when one of them is fetched in the run, when one came after it (the manifest's own times), or when it is a month old. `PartRef` gains the source `'wikidata'` ("Wikipedia's other editions near …"), which `GIVE_UP` counts as a source. `SIZES.wikiMore` (12 KB an edition read past the first, from the real check's stored files at Tokyo Station) and `estimate` read `editions`. `loadPack` reads the rows into the articles of their editions; `Pack.articles` carries `also`; `Pack.pages` writes each held edition's cut summary and address, joined by " · ".
- Why we had to change: the main session's call 1 on the editions design's question, the user's "both." (2026-11-19): offline as online, an article with each edition's summary.
- Original design was wrong because: a part holds each edition's nearest 20, and those differ, so few articles came in two editions (Tokyo Station, Japanese and English: 2 of 38), and "both" offline needed what no list gives.
- Lessons set aside, and held: `format` stays 1 (a pack may be someone's only offline copy, as on 2026-11-19); a pack without the file reads as missing ("Download the rest", and on Wi-Fi the keeper brings it, its lists not asked again). "A format's list of kinds holds every kind its readers tell apart": the file is a kind of its own, because the release before reads a `wikipedia` file with no `edition` as English rows; that release's `readManifest` drops a kind it doesn't know, so it never reads these rows, and its download removes the file (its plan doesn't want it), which this one fetches again. Lesson 2026-11-19, "named by both": the edition set is in the path, so a language added or taken off is a new file and the old one goes, and a reorder stores nothing.
- Lesson: a file made from other cached files is fresh only while it is newer than each of them, read from the times the record already keeps: a rebuild that failed after its sources came back then leaves nothing to remember, and the next run sees it stale; and a file an older reader would misread as one of its known kinds takes a kind of its own, which the older reader drops at its manifest.

## 2026-11-20 A file named by its inputs keeps its former one until the new one comes
- Changed (fix round on editions-b-next): `fetchParts` in download.js keeps a Wikipedia part's former second-stage file (`/offline/wikipedia/<id>.x.*.json`, any set of editions) in the manifest while the part's new file is not in `files`: after a language was added or taken off and the run stopped or failed before the new stage came. The reader already read any `wikipedia-editions` file and kept the rows of the editions read, so nothing there changed. The old file goes at the download that brings the new one, as a file no longer wanted does; with English alone no stage is wanted and it goes at once.
- Why we had to change: the stage's path carries the editions read (lesson 2026-11-19, "named by both"), so a changed set made the old file unwanted, and the manifest write removed it whatever the run got. The final review reproduced it at Yufuin: a stopped run after Chinese was added took 9 rows with two editions' summaries to 2, and so did Chinese taken off with Wikidata answering 503.
- Original design was wrong because: it treated "named by both" as "the old one is junk the moment the name changes". It is junk only once its successor exists; until then it is the best the pack has, and "a file this download doesn't get stays as it was" is the rule everywhere else (`fetchParts`, the guides).
- Lesson: a derived file whose name carries the set of inputs it was made from is a new file when the set changes, and the former stays, read as it is, while the new one is missing, and goes at the download that brings it.

## 2026-11-20 An answer of rows and endings fits its tool result by dropping rows, never endings
- Changed (fix round on editions-b-next): nearby's table and its endings (the pack and its day, what the pack holds and lacks, the reach line, OpenStreetMap's cut line) are built together, and over `RESULT_MAX` (tools.js, 40,000 characters) the farthest article rows are left out one at a time until the whole fits, the reach line saying how far the rest reach (`Listed.cut`, `reach`). One rule at every return, online or from the pack. The same round gave the endings their words: `Pack.lacks(editions)` (read.js, a boolean: false under English alone, else whether a Wikipedia part has no second stage for exactly these editions, or no list of one of them, each read by part and edition: a stage kept as the former one outlives the lists of an edition taken off, so a language put back between downloads leaves stages over a part with no list of it, and a stage is no proof of its lists) and nearby's lines from it (the holds line, said whenever an article of the answer came from the pack and true whatever came, with no number and no edition, as a second stage holds more than a list's 20 of an edition and a list under its cap holds all its geosearch gave: "holds only the Wikipedia articles near each stop and every 15 km of the way, not all of Wikipedia, so Wikipedia may have more than these"; and, after it where `lacks` is true, one constant sentence that a row may leave out an edition that has its article, naming no edition and promising no download); "Wikidata wasn't asked, as the time had run out" where the shared limit had already run out in the lists (it was "didn't answer"); the reach line leaving the places out when OpenStreetMap's answer was cut at 400 (its own line says so); and one clause when the radius is over 10 km, Wikipedia's geosearch limit (`GEOSEARCH_MAX_M`, wiki.js, the one place the number is written).
- Why we had to change: tools.js cuts a result at 40,000 characters from the end, and the endings are the end. A pack's rows hold each edition's summary and address, so four editions sit at the limit and en+de+fr with every article in each come to about 43,000 (the final review's synthetic pack, 2026-11-20): the cut took the farthest rows and every ending, so Shiori would present the rows as the whole answer from the whole pack. The builder's proposed known limitation (and a rule dividing each summary by the editions) would have left the answer cut.
- Original design was wrong because: it measured one case (three editions, 23,346 characters) and judged the limit far enough off; the limit is the reader's, not the answer's, and the answer's size grows with a setting the traveller chooses.
- Lesson: where a result limit cuts the end of a text, an answer whose rows can grow drops its farthest rows until it fits, through the cut line it already has, so what the limit would have taken (where it came from, what it lacks, how far its rows reach) is still there; the number is the limit's own.

## 2026-11-20 A reminder is shown before its entry is written, and a write nobody waits on says so

- What changed (storage-rest-next; the storage-rest design of 2026-11-20, its parts 1 to 3): `Agent.remind` shows each reminder's card and says it (voice on) before it writes its entry; a refused entry is told and stops nothing, so `remind` no longer rejects for its write (`Halves.remind` returns Kokoro's). A turn that stops (its setup or its steps throw: a read of its voice or rules, a refused write of its start, of a line joining it, of its end) shows what it still owes (as the page is being left it doesn't, and the next open shows what the log still owes, since "2026-11-20 A wake's record keeps the reminders it carries"; on a full phone, a reminder due in that instant is C172) before the failure goes on to `#drain`'s card, from one place (`#turn`'s `#showOwed`): the list the turn keeps (`#owedNow`), taken once (the end's own `remind` takes it first, a line that joins puts its reminders in it before its write), else its wakes' own when it stopped before it had one; a wake queued behind it keeps its own and gets its own turn at once (a line typed for it goes back to the box instead), which shows them if it stops too (`#drain` drains again: "2026-11-20 What waited for a conversation left goes with its card, and the line of a move is written whether or not the open worked", below). `wakeFailed` shows a cue too, as a turn's end does when no model answered. `#keepWakes` (what waited for a conversation the user left, kept as another opens) shows each scope's reminders first; a refused write there is told and what it couldn't keep is dropped, typed lines handed back, each scope tried (the entry below). A wake that fails before a turn has it (the budgets' read in `admit`, the halves' route) shows its reminders and tells the failure (`Agent.wakeFailed`). The writes nobody waits on that carry something for Shiori or the traveller are told through `#notSaved(e)`: a warn notice, "Something for Shiori wasn't saved", that carries the error and never rejects. `#keepFor`'s catch, which swallowed, and `#stopWakes`', which only logged to the console, call it, as a refused reminder entry (`remind`), a refused keep (`#keepWakes`, and the failed open's own keep in `open`) and a wake that failed before a turn (`wakeFailed`) do; main.js shows the storage card in its place on a full phone (`explainNotice`), and none for an error the dock showed already (typed words given back: `explainStorageFull` shows an error's card once). Typed words no write kept go back to the dock's box through `#unsaved` (the dock shows the storage card for a refused write), and a turn's own failure (`#drain`'s "Shiori stopped") and a failed open (`carryOn`'s "That conversation didn't open") carry their error to the same card. Not every write nobody waits on is told yet: the ones that still fail quietly on a full phone are pointed at in the Lesson below, as open work (the Deferred entry "Some writes still fail quietly on a full phone"). `Halves.carryOn` (Open on annai's card) tells a failed open with a notice titled as `tellNotOpen`'s, "That conversation didn't open", the error carried, in place of a console line; `open_trip` writes `conversation.moved` after it whatever the open found (the entry below: the fix rounds' gate on it went). In her own turn the open ends her call, and `#runCall` records MOVED whatever the open found. The swallowing catch of a failed open's own keep of the lines typed meanwhile (`open`'s `#keepElsewhere`) calls `#notSaved` too. `Halves.idle()` also waits for what a cut left unsaid to be shown (`#left`, which Start fresh already waited for), so idle() waits for a reminder's entry: it is now written after its card, and without that wait idle() settled before the entry was written. The reminders annai shows without anyone waiting for them (a wake held for quiet hours or skipped for the hour's limit, one shown while her question waits, one a Stop shows, the halves' route when the page is out of view or no session can take the wake) are counted by what idle() waits on (`#checking`; Halves' `#sending`), their entries written before it settles. The one case the typed-line hand-back had accepted (a line typed while its conversation was opening, queued behind a refused reminder) is gone: the reminder no longer fails the open before `#logWakes` hands the line back.
- Why we had to change: the storage-rest design check (2026-11-20, unit probes on a store refusing as the test kit's does; nothing ran on a phone) found that on a phone refusing writes, a reminder coming due while annai is on screen was neither shown nor said: `remind` wrote its entry first and threw before its card, and a reminder riding a wake for the open conversation died with the turn's first write, before the turn's owed list existed. Spec §11.3 promises the fallback "is shown or spoken directly", and a traveller can retype a line but can't see again a reminder they never saw. The writes nobody waits on were silent too: a file wake, a files-only hand-over or a watch's wake for a conversation that isn't open, refused, gave no card at all (`#keepFor` swallowed); a tap gathered and then stopped by a Stop gave a console line (spec §17's promise then, "It shows for any write the browser refuses for lack of room", never showed there; the spec now names the writes it tells); and annai's card's Open whose open failed showed nothing, while the tool told her the conversation was open. The reviews of the first build (2026-11-20) found the same loss in more places, and the fix round closed them: a turn's later writes (a line joining it, its end) refused before the reminders it owed were shown, a wake that failed before any turn had it (a read refused in the budgets' check or in the halves' route), the same Open's `conversation.moved` line written after a failed open, and idle() settling before the entries of reminders annai showed without anyone waiting for them were written. The re-review of that round (2026-11-20) found the rest of the family: `#keepWakes` stopping at the first conversation whose write was refused, so a typed line queued for the next one stayed in memory (lost at a reload; with Koe on, every line typed after the split); a reminder in a wake queued behind a turn that stopped, shown at no turn; a turn that failed in its setup, before its steps, showing nothing (Koe on and voice on, after Kokoro's refused first write); the Open's move line missing when only Koe's half failed to open, though the screen was in the trip; a reminder shown twice when the page came back while a session opened and none could take the wake; and Start fresh's reminder in Koe uncounted by idle(). The rounds' fixes for a refused keep (kept in memory, failing the open) and for the move line (gated on the screen) drew a finding in each round, and the step back's design check took both out (the entry below).
- Original design was wrong because: it wrote the record of an act before the act, so the record's failure took the act with it, for something that has no other way to reach the traveller; and it left a catch with nobody behind it empty or to the console, as if no one needed to know, where the failed-write card already had a place to go.
- Lesson: what the program says itself, on its own schedule, is said first and recorded after: the record is evidence, and its failure costs the record and a card that says so, never the saying; a turn that stops short (as the page is being left it doesn't, and the next open shows what the log still owes, since "2026-11-20 A wake's record keeps the reminders it carries"; on a full phone, a reminder due in that instant is C172), or a wake that never reaches one, says what it carried or still owes before its failure goes on, once, through `remind`. A write nobody waits on that carries what was meant for Shiori or the traveller (a wake, a line, a reminder's entry) tells its failure where it is caught, through `#notSaved` (one error, one card, which main.js turns into the storage card on a full phone), never an empty catch or a console line. That is true of the writes routed there and to the storage card as the entry above names them, not of every write yet. Other writes nobody waits on still fail quietly on a full phone (a spoken line's route and Koe's live lines among them): open work, the Deferred entry "Some writes still fail quietly on a full phone", which lists them by a search, since the list written here by hand came up short in every review round. Annai's own records of itself keep a quiet catch by design, and this change leaves them so, among them the voice's `state` record (`#voiceChanged`, loop.js), Kokoro's block for a live session's state (`Halves#block`), the setting of the open trip (`carryOn`'s `updateAppSettings`), the request trace's records (`net.js`). Moving a record after the act moves what waited on it: a wait for "everything done" waits for the work itself, not for the step that used to come last.

## 2026-11-20 What waited for a conversation left goes with its card, and the line of a move is written whether or not the open worked

- What changed (storage-rest2-next; the step back's design check of 2026-11-20, its option 1; chief's decisions C170 to C172, not the user's words): `#keepWakes`, which writes what waited for a conversation the user left as another opens (and, with nothing open, what came for `me`), tells a refused write (`#notSaved`) and drops what it couldn't keep, as `#keepFor` already dropped a wake for a closed conversation: each scope's reminders are shown first and taken off their wakes, which say annai showed them (`shown`, so a wake that is kept tells her turn there "(annai has already shown the user its reminder.)"), its typed lines go back to the box (`#unsaved`), and it never throws for a write. So `Agent.open` and `Halves.open` no longer reject for a refused keep, only for what is the open's own: a failed read, and its own repair of the tool calls a cut turn left open, whose write a full phone refuses (the Deferred entry "A tool call whose result the phone refused…"). A refused keep fails no open, nothing waits in memory, and an app update isn't held. `#drain` drains again after a turn that took wakes threw, as every other end of a busy spell already did (housekeeping's, an open's, Start fresh's `letGo`), so a wake queued behind a stopped turn gets its own turn at once (a line typed for it goes back to the box, from `#drain`'s catch as before: `#unsaved`); the drain again is guarded on a turn having taken wakes (the queue is then shorter, so it ends), since a throw before that could come again at once. `#showOwed` no longer takes the reminders of queued wakes: they are their own turn's, and a cue among them reaches a model when that turn works. `Halves.carryOn` returns nothing again, and `open_trip` writes `conversation.moved` after it whatever the open found: the line links to that conversation and says where the lines carried went (they are written where the phone takes their writes; a full phone refuses those, and the line's own write, alike), and annai's card says an open that failed. A refused move line gives no card of its own yet (open work: the Deferred entry "Some writes still fail quietly on a full phone"): in her own turn it is written after the open ended her call (`logIn`'s bare `record`), where `runTool`'s race has already returned, so a refused move line is told by no card: the cards of the same Open (the failed open's, the lines' keep's, a stopped turn's) show only their own failures; and after a failed open the line may show only at the chat's next draw, as `record` emits no event.
- Why we had to change: RULES' step back after the storage-rest-next rounds (a Fable design check, tried in a scratch copy on the kit's refusing store) asked of each part the findings kept landing in whether it was needed. Two were not. The waiting: a refused keep for a conversation not open was kept in memory, to be written once there was room, and the first refusal failed the open; while it waited, every other open failed too (by the design check's reading of the code, not run: the conversation screen then drew the conversation still open under the tapped one's address), an app update waited, and a restart lost it anyway; the reviews of round 2 found three more patches it would need (a queued cue shown as a card before any model had it, a kept reminder that her turn could say again, a later conversation's refusal never told). A wake left behind a stopped turn waited for a next wake that might never come, as `#drain`'s failure was the one end of a busy spell that didn't drain again. The gate: `carryOn` returned whether the screen was in the trip, read from the halves' targets, and each round's fix of it made the next finding (the line missing when only Koe's open failed, then written when both failed and Koe's failed first). The gate mattered only when reads fail and writes work, which neither full-phone shape gives (there the line's own write is refused), and what it held back was a true and useful line.
- Original design was wrong because: it kept what a refused write couldn't keep in memory for a retry that only a narrow window could use (room back before a restart), at the cost of every step that passed it meanwhile; it made opening one conversation depend on writes for others; and it gated a record of what happened (the lines went to Kyoto) on whether the open worked, an answer that isn't there when the record is due: in her own turn the open ends her call, whose result is written without it, and with two halves the gate read the screen when the first half's open failed, the other still opening.
- Lesson: a keep (what waited for a conversation, written into it) that is refused is told and what it couldn't keep is dropped (typed words handed back), never held in memory for a retry: the retry serves a window nobody sees, and the held state fails every step that passes it (each open, an update). An end of a busy spell after a failure goes on with the wakes that waited, as every other end does, once the failure has made the queue shorter (a typed line goes back to the box instead). A record of what happened is written whether or not what was meant to follow it worked, never gated on whether an open worked, an answer that isn't there when the record is due; its own refused write gives no card of its own yet (open work: the Deferred entry "Some writes still fail quietly on a full phone"), and after a failed open it may show only at the chat's next draw. API changes: `Agent.open` and `Halves.open` resolve after a refused keep, and `Halves.carryOn` returns nothing.

## 2026-11-20 A wake's record keeps the reminders it carries

- What changed (owed-leave-next, with its fix rounds 1 and 2; the Deferred entry "A reminder owed as the page is left isn't shown at the next start", its own fix): `#logWakes` writes `reminders` on a wake's record, the reminders its wakes carried, each as its entry keeps it (`watch`, `text`, and `answer` or `plan` where it has one; one function, `reminderData`, makes both, so the read matches an entry by watch and words), never a cue, and no key when it carries none. A turn's meanwhile record is written for a wake that joins with reminders and no line of its own too (Halves' block for Kokoro when Koe is down, `kind: 'halves'`, logged already: its `reasons` empty). `Transcript.owedReminders` reads them back by the loop's own rule (`#steps`' settle, and `remind`): a reminder is settled by its `reminder` entry, or by her reaching the user after it in the half of the newest record that carries it, as each half's `#reached` counts only its own lines (a turn that joins a wake writes its reminders again in its own meanwhile record, so the half that joined it settles them). What reaches the user is one predicate, `reaches` (speak, show, ask, a reply shown as written; never annai's own card), exported by transcript.js and used by the loop's count (`#write`) and by the read. `grounded` joins `SAID`, so a load reads the reply shown as written; housekeeping's measure (`compact`) leaves it out as it leaves `out.*`, so a reply counts once, in its `model.turn`. `Agent.open` shows what `owedReminders` still holds, once the transcript is read, through `remind` (the card, said with voice on, then its entry, which settles it), in any scope and whatever the transcript says of the turn, as `#keepWakes` shows a queued wake's; with two halves Kokoro's half shows them, and Koe's does not (on a first open Halves starts Koe's open only after Kokoro's is done, so Koe's read follows what Kokoro wrote; on a later open Koe's runs beside Kokoro's, and its restart waits for Kokoro's open only when Koe's log is unanswered or repaired). At a start the card waits for the screen: main.js holds the agent's `notice` and `clear`, in order, from before the open until the shell is up, as it does for the push mirror's cards. An entry written into the conversation being opened is told to the other half too (`#logIn`'s record path calls `logged`, as `#write` does for the open conversation), so Koe's copy of the log knows the reminder was shown. A restart wake carries `shown: true` when its half's own read still held the reminders at its open (her wake's words then say annai already showed them). On a first open, when the phone took Kokoro's entry, Koe's read follows it and its restart carries none, reading the entry itself; when the phone refused it, Koe's read still holds the reminder and its restart carries `shown: true`. A restart never carries the reminders as `fallbacks`. A turn that ends while the page is being left writes its `turn.end` as ever. A record from before has no key and reads as none (no migration); an older build ignores the field.
- Why we had to change: on any phone, a turn that owed a reminder (a watch's "Leave for the airport by 14:10") and ended or stopped as annai was closed or put away showed nothing there (a card on a page being left reaches no one), and the next start carried nothing: her model was told what fired, so she said it if she spoke, but if she slept without a word, or couldn't reach her model, the reminder was gone. The build's first route had the restart wake carry the reminders as its fallbacks, and a turn that ended by itself while the page was being left write no end so that the next start took it up. Review found what that route cost: the restart is queued only for a turn the log holds unanswered, so a turn with an error written for it after the cut (a Stop at that instant, a budget, a quiet card) or an end written before its show lost the reminder all the same; the restart's cue said "in the middle of your turn" for a turn that had ended by its sleep; and with Koe on, the restart turn could not settle a reminder Koe said, so annai showed it too. Showing what the log owes at the open depends on none of those.
- Original design was wrong because: what a wake owed the traveller lived only in the running turn's memory (`owed`), while its record kept only what it told her model; the take-up read the cut turn's other duties back from its records (that it was unanswered, the hand-over it carried) but not that one. And the first fix hung the show on a model turn that may not happen (the restart), and on how the cut turn ended, when the log already says what is still owed: what a person is owed is shown by whoever opens the work, not by the work being taken up. The build's first read kept a copy per record and dropped repeats as it answered, which left the copy of the half that didn't take it up owed after the other half settled it: whoever carries a thing last is the one who owes it.
- Lesson: what cut work owes a person is written on the work's own record as the work takes it on, and whoever opens the work again shows what the log still owes, at once, by the rule the live work settles it by (an entry, or a reach after it in the same half), not through the take-up of the work itself, so how the work ended can't lose it. Where the live loop and the read remain two writers of the rule, they share what they can (the predicate `reaches`) and a test keeps the rest in step; they are not one answer. Whoever carries a thing last owes it. A full phone refuses the entry, so such a reminder shows again each time its conversation opens (C169, widened by C174): no state is kept to stop it. What is shown at a start is held until something can show it, never emitted into nothing. A new field old records lack needs no key on new records that have nothing to say, when old and new read alike as none. API changes: the `wake` record's `reminders`; `Transcript.owedReminders` (a field) and `reaches` (an export); `SAID` holds `grounded`; a restart wake may carry `shown: true`; `Agent.open` writes a `reminder` entry and raises a card for what the log still owes; main.js subscribes to the agent's cards before the open.

## 2026-11-22 A reminder held in memory is written as it is first held

- What changed (remind-log-next; the Deferred entry "A reminder held only in memory is lost when annai is closed", its own fix): whatever holds a watch's reminder in memory before a turn's `wake` record carries it writes it into its conversation's log as it is first held, through one method, `Agent.hold`: a `reminder.held` record, `{ reminders, page }`, its reminders as a wake's record keeps them (`reminderData`, now exported; never a cue). The loop's queue holds each wake it takes in (`#push`, so a wake waiting behind her running turn, or for its gather, is in the log), and Halves holds what it hands Koe's live session (in Koe's half, before the session's own wait for Koe's open; the session may keep it until Koe's turn ends, or until the page is back in view).
