The live session writes its own verdict where it decides: `reminder.told`, `{ reminders }`, as it passes reminders on (`LiveKoe#passed`, after its one rule `#passes`, which now also holds the check that her words began after the notes went in; a card or question passes only what was in flight when its call came, never a reminder that went in while the call ran), and nothing as it takes them in; the places that show a reminder instead of passing it on (a tap, a cut-in, `already_told`) take it out first, so only a real pass is written. `Transcript.owedReminders` reads a held one as owed until its entry, a `reminder.told` that names it (by watch and words, as an entry; never owed itself), or the record of the turn that takes it on, which the existing rule then settles; no line of hers settles a held copy. Each held copy keeps the page's mark (`held`), and `Agent.open` shows what the log owes except what the opening page holds itself: that is its own to say (the page mark alone decides, `held === #page`, never whether the holder is alive); every copy another page holds is shown, that page dead or still open, and a tab another tab takes annai over from has its turn's reminders and its live session silenced at the takeover (`leaving()`, `hush('away')`). A Stop that drops queued wakes shows their reminders silently, as Start fresh does (`Agent.remindDropped`): the mark hides a copy from this page's open, so nothing else would show it; and it writes down as stopped what the user sent among them (`Agent.stopDropped`), as a Stop of a gathering turn does, where it had dropped it unwritten, in the conversation they were sent for (the Stop picks them by the conversation being opened, and writes there as an entry for a conversation being opened is written, `#logIn`, which that open takes into the transcript it reads). `page` is one mark for both halves (Halves gives both halves the same mark; a standalone Agent makes its own), a random id, never the notes' nonce. `SAID` holds `reminder.held` and `reminder.told`; the Trace lists both under GPS with their words. A refused `reminder.held` is quiet: the page still says the reminder and, on a full phone, its entry's refusal, when it is shown, brings the storage card; only one that then dies loses it, as before.
- Why we had to change: on any phone, a reminder that a wake carried while it waited in memory (behind her running turn; for Koe's session to say it; with the page out of view) was gone when the page died: no record carried it, its `trigger.fired` said it had fired, so it never fired again, and the next open showed nothing (owed-leave-next's final review, finding 3, by a hard kill; the Koe · Realtime case was the builder's). This change's first build had the live session write Koe's `wake` record as it took a reminder in, with `page`, and the read settle that copy by Koe's lines; its two fix rounds and a re-review kept finding a line the read counted as her answer and the session did not (a cut line, a line before a lookup she speaks after, a bare word, her line ending as an urgent reminder cut in), each of which lost the reminder when the page was hidden and then killed, so a design check replaced the record and the line rule with the session's own verdict.
- Original design was wrong because: what the page owed the traveller reached the log only when a turn took it on, so memory was its only record in between, which the page's death erases; and the open, which shows what the log owes, had no way to tell what another page held from what the live page still holds and will say; and the first fix had the read work out from the shape of Koe's lines what the session had decided, a second judge beside the session's own rule (`#passes`): each round found a shape where the two disagreed.
- The route not taken: the final review's, reading `trigger.fired` (it keeps the watch and the words) as owed until a carrier or an entry, adds no record, but it is written before the decisions that leave a firing's reminder out (a catch-up drops a leave reminder when it fires the item's arrival too; a notification the phone showed; a cue), so each of those would need a record of its own to settle it, and it keeps neither the plan's words nor a half; a record written where the reminder is held carries exactly what will be said, after every decision.
- Lesson: what a program holds in memory to do for a person is written as it is first held, by the holder, as a record no unrelated act settles: only the act's own record, or the record of the work that takes it on. An earlier record that already names the item serves only when nothing between it and the hold can drop the item. A reader that shows what the log still owes leaves to the opening page what it holds itself, by its own mark (its page), never by time or by whether a holder is alive: what another page holds is shown, and a holder that must not say it too (a tab taken over) is silenced where it is taken over. The holder that decides writes its verdict (for a live session, `reminder.told`: it passed the thing on) where it decides; for a copy held in memory a reader takes that record, never the holder's lines (a turn's `wake` copy keeps the existing rule: her line after its record settles it). API changes: the `reminder.held` record; the `reminder.told` record, which a live session writes as it passes reminders on; a live session writes no `wake` record as it takes reminders in (this change's first build did, with `page`; a log that holds one reads it by the plain rule, Koe's lines settling it); `Agent.hold`; `reminderData` exported; `Half.page`; `Agent.remindDropped`; `Agent.stopDropped`; an `owedReminders` item may carry `held`.

## 2026-11-22 A plugin's words are blanked where it hands them to annai, and only what the docs offer comes through
- Changed (plugin-words-next, with its fix round 1; the Deferred entry "A third-party plugin's words outside a thrown error reach Shiori or the log unblanked"): for a plugin that isn't built into annai (installed, or from annai's catalog), four documented calls changed what they hand on. `annai.tools.register`'s `run` comes back as a result object: its text blanked of every key annai keeps, its pictures and document as `mediaType` with `data` or `hash` only, and `isError`; a picture's or document's `path`, `name`, `region` and `page`, `heard`, `endTurn` and `told` are left out. `annai.events.append` keeps `data` as blanked plain data, its object keys and its texts blanked too (a `Date` as its ISO text, as JSON keeps it); the `type`, the text names and the blobs are kept as given. `annai.wake(reason)` blanks the reason, so it resolves after a store read (a built-in's stays synchronous). A search provider's rows are blanked, and its `search()` runs on its caller's `this`, where `ready()` ran. While the keys can't be read, each of those is the note. Behind them, net.js: `withoutSecretsIn` walks an object's names too (kept as they are while the keys can't be read, since every name made a note would be one name); `decodeSafe` decodes each run of `%XX` on its own, so a stray "%" ("100%") no longer switches off the "held a key" check; the check looks through four decodings, and blanking covers a key encoded once or twice; the Bearer and Basic shapes are narrower (a Bearer value must hold a digit or be a JWT, a Basic value must be base64 of UTF-8 text holding ":"), because the shapes now run over ordinary answers ("Basic Economy/Main Cabin" had become "Basic «key» Cabin"). `annai.secrets.blank` gets the same names and shapes.
- Why we had to change: a plugin may repeat its provider's answer, the address with its key in it, and only a tool's thrown error, a crash's log entry and the PDF pages plugin's error were blanked; a tool's text, a search row, a wake's reason and a log entry's words reached Shiori and the log with the key. The first build passed a plugin's whole result on after blanking its text, which let `heard` and `endTurn` through (a plugin could end her turn) and a picture's path, which can carry a signed address; and the shape rules that were fine on error words were wrong on answers.
- Original design was wrong because: it blanked words at the few sinks it knew (a thrown error, a crash's log entry) of the several places a plugin's words come in, and kept a plugin's result as an open object, so whatever a plugin added came through; a walker that kept object names left a map keyed by address; a decoder that gave up at one stray escape gave up on the whole text; and a shape rule written for an error's words was run over every answer without asking what its credential looks like.
- Lesson: blank where a plugin's words first reach annai (the host's API, once for every call), not at each place they land (only what can't pass it, a thrown error or a trigger's synchronous check, is blanked where it is written), and pass only the parts the docs offer: copy those fields out of what a plugin returned, never spread it, so a field the host reads for itself (`heard`, `endTurn`) can't be set from outside. Names of an object are text. A decoder works on each run it can decode, not the whole text. A rule that reads words for credentials carries the structure of its credential (base64 of user:password; a token holds a digit), since it also runs over ordinary answers. A wrapper hands the call on with the caller's `this`. API changes: those above; `annai.secrets.blank` and `docs/plugins.md` say so.

## 2026-11-23 A write nobody waits on says so wherever it is caught, through the one card it already has
- Changed (quiet-writes-next; the Deferred entry "Some writes still fail quietly on a full phone"): `Agent.notSaved(e)` is public (one line over `#notSaved`, the card "Something for Shiori wasn't saved" with the error, which never rejects), and `Halves.notSaved(e)` hands it to Kokoro's, so the writes nobody waits on outside the loop tell their refusal through the card and the route they already had (Halves' notices, which main.js turns into the storage card on a full phone): halves.js `#leftUnsaid`, `#alongside` (a wake's way to a live session shows its reminders too, through `wakeFailed`), `#koeOpen`, `#tapFollow`, `#settleAside`, `#note`, a note's way to a live Koe (`deliver.koe`'s `#live`, which had no catch at all), and the five routes that carry something for the user or Shiori (`#backstop` for a session's unsaid notes and `#handOn`, `#koeFailed`, `#dropped` when cut, a tap as annai starts, each passing `#gone`); koe/live.js `#write` (through `this.c.koe.notSaved`); trip-tools.js open_trip's `conversation.moved` after her call ended (through `app.notSaved`). In the loop, `#heard`'s route, `#cutSpeech` after a restart and `#afterTurn` call `#notSaved` in place of an empty catch or a console line. A refresh-only route keeps its console line, and annai's own records of itself (`#block`, Kokoro's block for Koe's state, the voice's state, the open trip's setting, the request trace, the device hint, `reminder.held`, a live session's Trace numbers: `live.turn`, `live.reply`, the live-silent notes; the four "unshown" Trace lines for what of Kokoro's annai didn't show) their quiet catch. The "unshown" lines go through one writer, `#unshown`, which catches at the write as `#block` does (quiet-records-next, the step back after the second fix round): three of them were written bare under `#backstop`'s and `#leftUnsaid`'s telling catches and showed the card for nothing of the traveller's, and the fourth had an inline catch of its own. Kokoro's block for Koe's state is written again by the next refresh that finds Koe's state other than the last one written (`#tell` puts back what it had told when the write is refused; a refresh that ran while the write was out returned early and doesn't write it) and never fails the route that carries something; a loop over several writes (`#backstop`, `#leftUnsaid`, a turn's chatter lines, speech a restart or a cut-in cut) tells each refused one and goes on to the next. Typed words still go back through `#unsaved`.
- Why we had to change: on a phone too full to write, a spoken line whose route failed, her words cut by a restart, a turn's end records, the notes between the halves, what annai shows of Kokoro's notes, a line or wake on its way to a live session, Koe's own open, a live session's lines and the chat's line of a move were gone with no card of their own; with no other write told in that moment the traveller saw nothing. Two were worse than quiet: a note to a live Koe whose session couldn't open, with Kokoro's block refused, was a rejection nobody handled; and a watch's wake given to a live session that couldn't open lost its reminder entirely (no card, no reminder).
- Original design was wrong because: the card that tells a refused write was private to the loop, so the writes outside it (the halves, the live session, a tool's run after its call ended) had no way to it and fell back on an empty catch or a console line; the list of what to tell was written by hand and came up short in every review round, so this change worked from a search (the entry's grep, 45 hits, each judged by what its promise writes) and read the callers of each hit it changed. The route default was first made to tell, and that told a refresh's failed read as a second card beside the failed open's own: a route is told by what it carries, not by having failed. The review found three more of the same kind: `#tell` set what Kokoro was told before its write, so a refused block was never written again and, thrown, stopped the route that carried the traveller's words (the answer, the tap, a drop's finish), though the block is only annai's record of Koe's state; the live session's one catch told its Trace numbers as if they were the traveller's lines; and a loop over several writes ended at its first refused one, so a later note's words or reminder never showed, and a refused chatter line ended a turn's step.
- Lesson: a write nobody waits on tells its failure where it is caught, through the one card the program already has for it, made reachable from every module that writes (one public method over the private one, never a second card), not through a path of its own; what it judges by is what the promise carries for a person, so a step that only reads state again, or writes the program's record of itself, stays quiet, and a step that fails before its hand-ons shows what it carried (a reminder) as well as telling. A record the program keeps of itself has one writer, which catches at the write (or at its one call, where the writer needs the refusal to put back what it was told), so no caller's catch decides it; it is written again at the next look that finds it out of step (what it was told is put back when the write is refused) and never fails what depends on it; a loop over several writes tells each refused one and goes on. A promise with no catch at all is the same bug, louder: read the callers of a helper you change for the ones that never handled it. API changes: `Agent.notSaved(e)`, `Halves.notSaved(e)`.

## 2026-11-23 One rule decides whose failure an error is, on every path that can blame a plugin
- Changed (cut-send-next, with its fix rounds 1 and 2): `isFetchFailure` (registry.js) is the one rule for whether an error an endpoint type's code throws is the link's or the plugin's, on the tool path (`isPluginBug`, a tool's thrown error) and on the endpoint path (call.js `asProviderError`: whatever the type throws in a call, a reply's read or a `run` type's own send, for chat, a transcription and a voice). A `TypeError` whose message names a fetch, the network or a failed load, by whole words (`/\b(fetch|network|load failed)\b/i`: "Failed to fetch", "Load failed", "network error", "The network connection was lost.", Firefox's "NetworkError when attempting to fetch resource"), is `network`, the link's: chat's rule retries it and never counts it against the plugin. Anything else thrown still counts as a bug in the plugin, a `TypeError` of its own that only names something with "fetch" or "network" in it (`this.fetchModels is not a function`, `reading 'networkState'`) included. The plugin-facing sentence is docs/plugins.md's "Errors" bullet for endpoint types, which says what the rule takes, from which errors, and that the words are whole words.
- Why we had to change: three dropped transcription replies in a row (a body that failed after its headers, read by the type's own `readTranscript`) switched the speech plugin off, and its voice with it, for something that was the link's. Round 1 fixed that by making the endpoint path take the browser's fetch failure as the link's, with the tool path's rule, which read `/fetch|load failed|network/i` on any `TypeError`: the re-review found that it took a plugin's own bug naming `fetchModels` or `networkState` for the connection too (shown as "The connection dropped.", sent again, never counted), on both paths.
- Original design was wrong because: the tool path already took the browser's fetch failure as an answer (§17) while the endpoint types' path blamed the plugin for everything its code threw, so one question had two answers by path; and the rule that answered it on the first path matched by a substring, which reads an identifier as the browser's words. The link's failures and a plugin's own bugs arrive as the same kind of error, with no code, so only the words tell them apart.
- Lesson: one rule decides whose failure an error is, on every path that can blame a plugin, never one per path: a path without it switches a plugin off for the link's faults, and a second copy drifts (the looser one wins its path). Where only words tell the two apart, match whole words, so a plugin's own longer names don't read as the link's (a bare `fetch` or `network` still does). The other rule, by position (the reply's read is the link's, whatever it throws, as sse.js does for a stream), is a design change, to be taken as one and not patched in.

## 2026-11-23 A transcription's retries are chat's one rule
- Changed (cut-send-next, fix round 2): `nextStep` (fallback.js) is exported and holds the offline test (no connection on the device: neither a wait nor a rest for a failure to connect or a dropped reply); `callRole`, `restOf` and the voice's `#hear` all take their retry step from it, so a transcription is sent again as chat is: a dropped reply or a 5xx twice (1 s, 3 s), an overloaded server twice (0.5 s, 1.5 s), a short rate limit once, and a dropped reply or a failure to connect not again while the device is offline. `Voice.retryMs` (a list of waits only the tests set) became `Voice.sleep`, a function, because the waits are nextStep's now; a Stop is checked first at every try of `#hear` and first in its catch, before any kind of failure.
- Why we had to change: `#hear` kept a kinds list and an offline-less loop of its own, a copy of callRole's retry rules, and each round found a gap in the copy: a Stop during a retry's wait (round 1), a Stop as the last try's error body was read, and a dropped reply retried while offline (round 2).
- Original design was wrong because: a second place decided whether to send again, with its own list of kinds and its own order of checks, so every case chat's rule handled had to be found in the copy again.
- Lesson: where two paths need the same decision (whether to retry, after how long, whether a failure rests a model), the second takes it from the first's function, never a copy of its rules with a list of its own; the one thing that differs (a test's wait) is the injected part (`sleep`), not the rule.

## 2026-11-23 An endpoint type's failure is decided by where it happened, and annai reads the reply
- Changed (fail-owner-next; the design check after cut-send-next's step back, fail-owner-design.md): on the endpoint path annai reads the reply's body for the type. `read`, `readTranscript`, `readSpeech` and `readSpeechStream` are given a Response annai made (call.js `watched`: the reply's headers over a body annai pulls from the real one as the type reads; its status is 200, its status text and `url` are empty and it has no byte-stream reader; a cancel reaches the real body. Neither status text nor status is copied, as the Response constructor refuses some of what a real reply has, and a throw there is inside callOnce's own try, which counted it as the plugin's: a reason phrase such as `200 成功` (round 1), then, found in Chromium and WebKit (Node's fetch gives a 204 or 205 no body, so a test in Node alone never saw one; round 2's test builds one by hand), a 204 or 205 that comes with a body stream and a status of 600 to 999 that browsers deliver (round 2); annai reads the real status itself), and annai notes in the call when its pull of the body fails. `asProviderError(e, signal, type, registry, dropped)` takes that note as a new last parameter and decides by position, in this order: the call's signal (timeout, then cancelled, whatever was thrown as it stopped), then the note (`network`, "The connection dropped.", the read's error as its cause, whatever the browser calls it: in Firefox annai's own read of a body cut mid-way fails with a TypeError and the type's `res.json()` with an AbortError, which the name clauses had read as a Stop), then an AbortError or TimeoutError the signal didn't cause, by its name (the type's own timer), then the type's own ProviderError, else a bug in its plugin, counted. An error reply's body is read through `watched` too, so one that drops is `network`, not the status's kind (a cut 400 had lost its `context`). call.js no longer imports `isFetchFailure`; it and `isPluginBug` stay the rule, by words, for the errors a plugin's own code throws wherever they are caught outside an endpoint type's call (the tool path, sign-in, backup, PDF pages, a provider's own list, a live protocol's open, a live trigger; a plugin's screen, dock action or avatar counts every error it throws, a link failure too, which is deferred); a request annai makes for the plugin (`annai.net.fetch`, a provider's `send`, the live socket) names its link failure by position. A speech type's request builders (`transcribeRequest`, `speechRequest`, `extraChars`) run inside the call's catch (`asProviderError`), and a chat type's `uploadRequest` inside `withUploads`' own catch: a bug in them is the plugin's, counted and traced (an upload's once, as uploads then stop at that endpoint and the photo goes as bytes; a result with no `url`, an async builder's promise, is the same bug). A typed address is checked by state before the call's own builders run (`build`, the speech builders, `uploadRequest`; the builders of the model list, a live open, the upload sweep and a kept turn check what they build, after it is built, and one of them that throws on the address first is read by its words where a counter takes it, a provider's list or a live open, and counts nothing in the sweep or a kept turn): `callOnce` (for a type that is not `run`) and `speechCall` call `sameOrigin(endpoint, endpoint.baseUrl, ability)` first, so an address that isn't an http(s) one (an empty one included: the provider page saves one) is a `bad-request`, said `no-address` ("has no working address. Give its address in Settings → Models.", the typed text never repeated; an older annai reading a record that holds it shows the kind's words), never counted, with no builder called, and a `new URL` that throws inside a builder is the plugin's bug, counted. `sameOrigin` says it for whatever it is asked when the ability's own address is no address, so the model list and a live session, whose builders run first and are checked after, say the same words (round 4: they said "built a request to an address that isn't one" and repeated the typed text), and it names the ability as the other said codes do (`saidAbility`: its description's name, else its kind's; `callOnce`, `speechCall`, `openLive`, the model list and Test pass it, a caller with none leaves the ability's id, `endpoint.ability`, as main did). `isBadAddress` stays, for `isPluginBug`'s paths (the tool path, a provider's list, sign-in, backup, live, PDF pages), where no call of annai's checks the typed address before the plugin's code runs, and reads four patterns (`invalid url`, `failed to parse url`, `cannot be parsed as a url`, `is not a valid url`): Chromium's "Invalid URL", WebKit's "cannot be parsed as a URL" and Firefox's "is not a valid URL" were measured in Playwright's builds, and Node's fetch says "Failed to parse URL from …" (a first version matched Chromium's and Node's words alone, so a WebKit error was counted). `guard` (a plugin's start, a hook) and `startInstalled`'s count of failed launches no longer count two shapes of a request that couldn't be made for want of a link, which the tool path never counted (`isPluginBug`): `isLinkFailure` is their one rule, the browser's own fetch failure (told by its words, as on the tool path), or an error of annai's kind `network` (what `annai.net.fetch` throws, agent/net.js) or `unreachable` (what annai's own sends throw), whoever threw it, so a plugin's own error of that kind is spared too; the error still reaches the caller, and an offline launch stays in the list of failed starts, so its card still says it didn't start (on a page no service worker controls, its name goes to the reload card). What else a missing link makes a start or hook throw still counts: annai's own `annai.llm` and `annai.llm.decide` throw an `AllFailedError`, which has no kind, and annai's own 10-second limit on a start throws a plain error. That is deferred, with its design check as the plan (docs/limitations.md). `registry.succeeded` is called where a type's call works (`callOnce`'s complete reply, `speechCall`'s whole reply and a stream's end), so a switch-off means three counted failures in a row, with none of the plugin's calls working in between. A `run` type makes its own requests, or none, and annai sends nothing for it, so it names their failures itself (a ProviderError of kind `network`). A voice's stall after its first piece is `stalled` whatever the type threw as annai's limit fired, read from `asProviderError`'s answer as chat's "paused" is. The plugin-facing sentences are docs/plugins.md's "The reply you read" and "Errors" bullets.
- Why we had to change: cut-send-next's three rounds of findings all landed in deciding by an error's words. A drop worded in a way no list holds (Node's "terminated", a DOMException) was counted against the plugin, three of them switching transcription off; a plugin's own TypeError naming a bare `fetch`, or saying "Failed to fetch", was taken for the link and never counted; a Gemini transcription whose reply dropped came out `server`, "Gemini's answer couldn't be read." (the plugin's catch took the read's failure and a bad JSON for one). The design check measured that Chromium replaces whatever error such a body ends with by its own `TypeError: Failed to fetch` in `json()`, `text()` and `arrayBuffer()`, a stop's AbortError too, so neither the words nor the error's identity can carry the fact through the type's code; and with the type's verdict read first, a Stop during a whole reply's read came out `network` there. Two rounds of fixes later the same part still drew findings (the copied status text, then the copied status; the address read by words, which missed WebKit), and each fix removed what the finding needed (the copy, and for the endpoint call the words) instead of adding a guard. Round 3 found the same kind of drift in two more places: the launch counter asked nothing about the link (it counted every error), where `guard` had a rule of its own that spared only the browser's fetch failure; and the name clauses read the type's own AbortError from a body Firefox cut mid-way as a Stop before the note, so the traveller saw "Stopped." and no other model was tried.
- Original design was wrong because: annai owns both places a link can fail on this path (it makes the send, and can own the body's read), yet it let the type read the raw body and then guessed from whatever the type threw; and the earlier lesson ("2026-11-23 One rule decides whose failure an error is, on every path that can blame a plugin") made the words the one rule on both paths, carrying the tool path's necessity (a tool's own code makes its requests) to a path that didn't have it.
- Lesson: wherever the host makes the request and reads the reply, decide whose failure an error is by where it happened, never by its words: the host reads the body for the callee and keeps the fact that its read failed in the call, never on the error, which a platform may replace; the call's own signal comes before any verdict a callee throws, then that fact (whatever the engine calls it), then an abort or timeout the signal didn't cause (read by its name), then the callee's verdict, and only then is anything else the callee's bug; a call that works ends the callee's run of failures. Words decide only the errors a plugin's own code throws, wherever they are caught outside such a call (the tool path, sign-in, backup, PDF pages, a provider's list, a live protocol's open, a live trigger: `isPluginBug`; a plugin's screen, dock action or avatar counts every error it throws, a link failure too, which is deferred); a request annai makes for the plugin (`annai.net.fetch`, a provider's `send`, the live socket) names its link failure by position. What the host builds for the callee carries only what the callee needs, here the headers: a field an engine's constructor may refuse (a status text, a 204's body, a status over 599) throws inside the call and is counted as the callee's. The typed address is checked by state, before the call's own builders run (`sameOrigin`), never by the engine's words for a bad one; the other builders check what they build, after it is built (one that throws on the address first is read by its words where a counter takes it). Where two counters ask "is it the link's" about one plugin (`guard`, the launch counter), they call one function (`isLinkFailure`). This corrects the earlier lesson for an endpoint type's call: its word rule covers only those other paths.

## 2026-11-23 A firing is made as it is delivered, and a wake is held as it first comes
- Changed (remind-first-next; the Deferred entry "A reminder in memory can still be lost in annai's first seconds"): a catch-up only collects what it finds due while it runs (its first check, then its wait for a GPS fix, up to `FIX_WAIT_MS`, 15 s), once per watch (a later look in the same catch-up finds it again and adds nothing), and makes those firings as it delivers them (`Live.#made`, the body `#fire` ran at once: the removed-trip check, `trigger.fired`, the watch's memory and last firing, a one-time watch's removal, the said-on-screen mark); a catch-up's `trigger.fired` records carry the time they were made, at delivery, and an `every` watch counts from there. The push relay's copy of what a catch-up has collected and not yet made is called off at once (`reminders()` leaves it out while the wait runs: the relay would send it in the wait, with sound), and the push mirror is told when the firings are made (`#endCatchUp`). `Agent.wake` and `Halves.wake` hold a wake's reminders (`reminder.held`, the same record) as they are given it (a wake for another conversation is written into its own log at once, with its reminders), before the budgets' check and before Halves' route (which can wait behind a conversation being opened), and mark the wake `held` (an optional field on the in-memory `Wake`, never written to the log), so the queue (`#push`) doesn't write them again and Halves' live branch no longer holds them itself. A Koe · Realtime card's call in a turn that was cut, whatever cut it (an urgent reminder, the user's speech, Kokoro's immediate note, a stop), passes nothing on (`#tool`'s `flight` is empty): that turn began before the notes went in.
- Why we had to change: three shapes remind-log-next left (its reviews and design check, 2026-11-22). A catch-up wrote each firing's `trigger.fired` and removed a one-time watch's file as it found it, then waited up to 15 s for a fix before its wake: a page killed in that wait had a log that said "fired", no watch, and nothing that carried the reminder, so it never came. A wake held nothing until a queue or a live session took it, after a settings read and, with two halves, after a route that waits behind an open under way. And a card's call already on its way when an urgent reminder went in counted as having told it.
- Original design was wrong because: the record that ends a duty (a watch spent and gone) was written when the firing was found, not when what it owed was handed on, so every step between the two was a window in which memory alone held it; the hold was placed at the queue, the first place the first build looked, not at the wake's first entry, so each route before the queue was such a window too; and a card was taken to answer whatever was in flight when its call arrived, though notes go in only between her turns or after cutting each one, so a cut turn's call was made before them.
- The routes not taken: marking the firing in memory at once and writing its record and the file's removal at delivery (memory and storage disagree for 15 s; a re-read of the watch files, which another watch's removal or a turn's watch set causes, brings the one-time watch back, and it fires twice, by reading; not built); holding the catch-up's reminders at its first look (the catch-up still decides after it: a leave reminder left out when the same catch-up infers the arrival would need a settle record of its own); holding at the entry and at the queue both (two or three records for each reminder); delivering first and then writing the firing (it closes the few milliseconds between the two writes too, but needs `wake` to settle once its hold is written, an API change, for a window already accepted as the very instant annai is being closed). A card's call that is the first thing of her turn to arrive after an urgent reminder went in can't be told on the page from one made for it: that needs the provider's own word (a cancellation, a reply id), and is left to a decision.
- Lesson: a record that ends a duty (here: "this watch won't fire again", and the watch's removal) is written in the step that hands on what it owes, just before the hand-on, never when a firing it still owes is found, and a step that collects for later (a catch-up's wait) only collects: what it decides is made at its end, so memory and storage never disagree while it waits. What holds a thing in memory holds it at its first entry, before every route after it, once, with a mark the later steps read so they don't write it again (as `logged` says a wake's lines are written). An act counts only for what was in flight as its call arrived, and for nothing if its turn was cut, whatever cut it: where inputs go in only between turns or after cutting the running one, a turn's cut mark says so. A mark that says the traveller could see a reminder (said on screen) has one write point, where annai makes it with the page in view, never where it gave a card no one saw: counted as said, that card would silence the relay's copy, the only notice that reached the traveller.

## 2026-11-24 The size rule weighs what a summary can make smaller, not the window's files
- Changed (size-forced-next; the Deferred entry "A PDF over the request size still brings a forced summary each of its turns"): what Settings → Models → Housekeeping's "Largest request (MiB)" (`maxRequestMiB`) weighs is narrower: the request's length without its pictures' and PDFs' data (in characters, as before: a Japanese or Chinese character counts one, three bytes on the wire; `bytesLessFiles`, compaction.js, replaces `requestBytes`, which counted a photo at its base64 size, an uploaded one as its id, and a PDF at its base64 size). The key, its range (1 to 64 MiB), its default (8 MiB) and its stored form are the same. What existed only for the old measure went: `requestBytes`' `uploaded` option and the loop's `#uploading()` that fed it, and the step loop's `summarized` flag (the size forced no other summary in a turn after the first). A request over the size by the window's files goes as it is, a photo or PDF in the window makes no housekeeping due by size (the cold start's check and the one after a reply leave them out too; each still counts 1,600 tokens toward the token rule), and a forced summary for words over the size can follow another in the same turn while the conversation since the last summary holds two or more exchanges (`cutAt` returns null once one exchange is left, so no model is asked then). A PDF over about three quarters of the size (its base64 size over the size) now stays whole in each step of its follow-up turn, unless a summary is forced there by words or tokens. Before this change, the summary forced there by the PDF's own size could take the PDF's own turn away (when the newer words filled the kept third before the cut reached it), and in the Deferred entry's probe did, so her follow-up lost the PDF it was about: that was the defect. The cost of mending it is bytes, 8.1 MiB sent in that probe where main sent 2.1 MiB, which is the user's question U116 in docs/progress.md and not changed here. The Settings sentence (and its three drafts), the README's long-conversations line and the spec's §6.4 and §6.5 say it. `llm.uploading` (service.js) has no caller in the app now; the uploads tests read the switch through it, so it stays until they can do without (Deferred: its own entry).
- Why we had to change: the user, 2026-10-19: "we are an agent on the go possibly with spotty internet. thus we shall make these optimizations when possible." Since the photo and PDF windows (2026-11-21 and 2026-11-22) a request holds its PDFs whole, and its pictures up to the model's count, for their turn and the follow-up. No summary takes this turn's files; the turn before's the size leaves out too, because a summary forced by their bytes could take them, and the follow-up would lose what it needs (one forced by words or tokens in the follow-up can still take them, and a read brings them back). So a request over the size by a PDF of about three quarters of it or more (its base64 size over the size) brought a forced summary in each of the PDF's two turns: in the first it left the request as large as it was, and while the cache was warm it carried the PDF whole once more (one more upload on a poor link); a PDF every model refused had that warm summary refused before annai's test of the PDF could run, so each turn ended in the "couldn't tidy up the conversation" card until the cache's hour ended; and in the follow-up it could take the PDF's own turn away, as it did in the Deferred entry's probe (a PDF of 1.5 MiB against a request size of 1 MiB): her follow-up lost the PDF it was about.
- Original design was wrong because: the size measured the request's length but could only act through a summary, and when the windows kept the newest files in the request (this turn's out of a summary's reach, the turn before's a loss to the follow-up if one took them) the measure was left as it was. Its build guarded the action's repeats instead: pdf-refused-next stopped the run of forced summaries with a flag, one a turn (`summarized`), which the "too long" summary didn't set (found by pdf-again-next's reviews), didn't stop the first one from being wasted, and didn't stop the warm one from sending the PDF whole; that one guard left the shapes of the next findings, because the measure was still counting what the action could not change. Routes not taken: dropping the forced branch (a conversation of words over the size would lose the one rule that holds the size the traveller set: with no window known the tokens never force a summary, and 1 MiB is about 300,000 tokens, far under 90% of a window of a million); and computing what `cutAt` would cut (more code for the one mixed case, words under the size and the window's files over it, which the token rule's due point still summarizes at a cold return or on the warm timer).
- Lesson: a trigger weighs what its action can change, and is re-based in the change that moves something out of the action's reach: the size could act only through a summary, and one forced by the window's files would make nothing smaller (this turn's) or could lose what the follow-up needs (the turn before's), so they are no part of what it measures. Guarding the action's repeats (a flag, a count a turn) leaves the measure wrong and each repeat a new shape. When the measure narrows, what the old one alone needed goes in the same change (the flag, the option that fed it, a test leg for a count that no longer exists), and a config key's Settings and README sentences and its spec say what it weighs now. API change: `maxRequestMiB`'s meaning (the key and its form are unchanged), `requestBytes` renamed `bytesLessFiles` without its `uploaded` option.

## 2026-11-25 A wake keeps the conversation it came for
- Changed (wake-scope-next; the Deferred entry "A reminder on its way when another conversation opens is shown again at the next start"): `Agent.wake` and `Halves.wake` give a wake the conversation it came for (the one open as it comes, unless it names one) at the moment they hold its reminders there, and where it lands, after the budgets' read (`admit`) or, with two halves, after Halves' route (its refresh, `llm.ready` at a start, the budgets' read inside it), a wake whose conversation is no longer the open one goes the way a wake for a conversation that isn't open goes (`#keepFor`): its reminders shown at once, and it is written into its own log. `#keepFor` and `#keepWakes` share one body (`#keepIn`), which shows a wake's reminders first and takes them off it (`shown`), as `#keepWakes` already did for a wake queued there when another opens; no earlier caller of `#keepFor` passed reminders. The budgets' own exits (quiet hours, the hourly limit) and `wakeFailed` show them in the wake's own conversation too.
- Why we had to change: remind-first-next's final review (F1, 2026-11-23, probed in both modes): a watch's wake for the open conversation was held there as it came, then given whichever conversation was open when it reached a queue, so a tap on another conversation, or on another trip's notification as annai's catch-up delivered, in that moment had Shiori tell the reminder in the other conversation (a misroute on main too), and the first kept its held copy, shown again, late, at the next start that opened it.
- Original design was wrong because: a wake's conversation was read where it landed, at the end of an async path (a settings read, a route that can wait behind an open), not where it came; the hold remind-first-next added at the wake's first entry took the conversation open then, so the two disagreed whenever an open came between them. An open's `#keepElsewhere` writes into their own conversations only the wakes already queued, so it could not catch one still on its way.
- The routes not taken: holding the wake back until the open is done and queueing it then (it would still need its own conversation to know where it belongs, and an open can take seconds); settling the held copy with a record written in the conversation left (a new record type, and the misroute stays); a check in `#push` for every caller (the live session, the landing the fix round found missed, has no queue of the agent's for it to reach; fix round 2's re-review tried the rest of it, below).
- Lesson: a step that waits between where it comes and where it lands takes its target where it comes, where it writes its first record, and lands there or, if that is no longer where the user is, by the path that already serves a target that isn't there, read again as it lands; never by a target read when it lands. The 2026-11-18 lines ("a step that runs later judges by the object it came from", "an answer that comes back later goes to … what it was asked about") said it, and the wake was a path they hadn't reached; it then took a fix round to reach every landing of it (below): a wait is a landing, wherever the code has one. API change: a wake given to `Agent.wake` or `Halves.wake` (a plugin's `wake`, Live's, the screens') belongs to the conversation open at the call, not at the end of its budgets' read; `Wake.scope` is set there.
- Fix round (wake-scope-fix1, from the final review of 2026-11-25, probed in scratch): the build put the check at the two entries (`Agent.wake` after the budgets' read, `Halves.wake` after its route) and left three landings reading the conversation open as they land. The live session's hand-on (`Halves.#live`) waits for Koe's own open and for a refresh, and a session still connecting when another conversation opens is closed at the switch, so it gave the wake to the session or to Koe's queue in the conversation opened (with Koe · Realtime: said there, its held copy shown again at the next start); its fallback showed an urgent reminder with no conversation named (so in the one opened); Live's delivery read the open conversation once, at its start, and gave the firing for it no conversation, so one listed after another's, whose notification was tapped meanwhile, was told in the one just opened. Done: `Halves.#live` asks one question, `#elsewhere`, the same as `Halves.wake`'s, after its waits (the second round made it every one: Koe's open, the refresh, the second model's connection), and shows a wake's reminders in its own conversation (or hands one that Kokoro was never given to Kokoro, who writes it there); Live gives `scope` to the wake and the reminder it hands on. With it, what the approved entry "A reminder can be written into both conversations when a switch lands while the live session connects" describes is: the silent copy the close writes and the copy `#elsewhere` shows after the refresh are both in the conversation left, none in the one opened.
- Routes not taken in the fix round: a mark that tells the fallback its reminder was handed on at the close, to keep the two copies to one (the approved entry's own fix: a new mark on a reminder, for a shape that now stays in the conversation it belongs to).
- Fix round 2 (wake-scope-fix2, from the re-review of the first, probed in scratch): the same rule at the places the first round's check did not reach. The session's hand-on (`Halves.#handOn`: voice goes off while the session connects with a wake, the close hands the session's copy on through a route that refreshes first) took the conversation open after that refresh, so another opened meanwhile was given the reminder (Koe's text turn there, or annai writing it there): the route now asks `#elsewhere` after its refresh, as `#live` does. `#backstop`'s wake branch names the wake's conversation (`w.scope`) for the same reason: the session's `unsaid` hook queues it as a route, and a switch can come before it runs. `#live` asks after the second model's connection too, so the check before `#talkDirectly` and the fallback's `w.scope` (a second guard behind the first) went. A third-party plugin's `annai.wake` read its conversation after annai had checked its words for keys (an asynchronous read of the secrets), so a switch in that moment moved it; the plugin host now reads it at the call and passes it as the wake's `scope`. An `aloud` wake (the recap) that no session takes while Koe stays live (today's spend reached) was written nowhere; `#live` hands it to Kokoro, as `#elsewhere` does for a moved one, so she writes it and answers it in text (a pre-existing gap, one line of the same helper).
- Routes not taken in fix round 2: keeping the two copies of a non-urgent reminder to one by letting `LiveKoe.wake` tell `#live` that the close handed its copy on (the condition already in the code: the close empties the waiting list): the copy it keeps is the silent one, so with voice on the traveller would hear nothing; the built shape (written twice in the conversation left, shown as one card, said once) is the better one. One place for the rule (each half's `#push` honouring a wake's `scope`) instead of a check at each landing: tried by the second re-review, with four checks removed (`Agent.wake`'s, `Halves.wake`'s after its route, `#handOn`'s filter, `#live`'s third): each half's queue then keeps its own copy of a moved wake (with Koe on a chat model, Kokoro's copy, saying Koe says the reminder, and Koe's were both written into the conversation left); with `Halves.wake`'s check kept, Kokoro is the one keeper again but N1's order writes a second wake record and Kokoro takes one more turn; and the live session's two checks before it is given the wake stay either way (it has no queue). The checks are one question, one helper per half (`here()`, `#elsewhere`), asked where a wake waited.

## 2026-11-25 Only a crash counts against a plugin wherever annai runs its code, through one door
- Changed (plugin-link-next; the design check after fail-owner-next's re-reviews 3 and 4 and RULES' step back, plugin-link-design.md; the Deferred entry "A plugin's start, hook or screen that fails for want of a link can still count against it"): every place where annai calls a plugin's code and reads no answer from it as it reads a tool's (a hook, a drawing through slots.js `mount`, a dock action through `runAction`, a component's `create()` through `made`, the dock avatar's calls, a live protocol's `open`, `attach`, `reopen`, messages and driver calls (fix round 2: a throw there that is no crash is now kept in the Trace too), and a heard-clips function; and a start, through `guard`, whose error goes on to `startInstalled` for the launch count and the card) passes one new registry method, `Registry.threw(pluginId, name, e)`: it always logs a `plugin.error`, and counts it only when `isPluginBug` says it is a crash (the tool path's rule: a `TypeError`, `ReferenceError`, `RangeError`, `SyntaxError`, `EvalError` or `URIError`, a `DOMException` named `SyntaxError`, `InvalidCharacterError`, `DataCloneError`, `HierarchyRequestError` or `IndexSizeError`, or a value thrown that isn't an `Error`, bar the browser's own fetch failure and an address `new URL` refused; the `URIError` and the five `DOMException` names came in fix round 1, which changes every caller of `isPluginBug`, not only the tool path (the tool path, PDF pages, backup, a provider's list, setup, sign-in, a live open, driver and message, and a live trigger): a plugin whose code throws one of them at any of those now counts); any other error neither adds to its plugin's run of failures nor ends it, and its log line has no `count`. `guard` calls it. The door takes what a call throws, and what a promise rejects with where annai awaits it (a start and a hook through `guard`, a drawing's and a dock action's). A live driver's call, a live message handler, an avatar's call and a heard-clips function are synchronous calls whose returned promise annai does not read: a rejection there is the page's "Something went wrong", counted nowhere. A `ProviderError` a protocol throws from a message is kept nowhere and ends nothing (`bug` leaves it out of the log because `open`, `attach`, `ready`, `reopen` and a driver call give it to a reader). `startInstalled`'s count of failed launches adds one only when what the start threw (the `cause` guard keeps) is a crash; an error guard didn't make (a module that doesn't load, a file or a manifest that's wrong, and whatever an entry module throws as it loads: a top-level `await` of the network that fails) counts as it is, whatever it carries as a cause (fix round 2: the cause is read only from the `PluginError` guard made, so a top-level `throw new Error('x', { cause: new TypeError('Failed to fetch') })` counts a launch). `isLinkFailure`, its export, its test and both its callers are gone. `registry.fail` stays for a verdict annai reached itself: an endpoint type's call by position, a crash on a path that hands the answer on to its reader (a tool, a provider's list, setup, sign-in, PDF pages, backup), a live trigger's check (live.js `#try`: its crash counted there, its plain error noted once in the conversation's log, nothing returned), the hooks' loop guard, and a component's `create()` that returned the wrong thing (`made` now counts that with `fail`, where it threw a plain `Error` into its own catch). annai's own 10-second limit on a start counts nothing (its error is a plain `Error`), as a tool's 150-second limit never did. The typed address is checked by state before a live protocol's `open()` (open.js `build`) and before any model list's code (service.js `listModels`, for a type that isn't `run`, as `callOnce` does): an address that doesn't parse is a `bad-request`, said `no-address`, and no plugin code runs. Words: spec §5.5; docs/plugins.md, "The entry module" (with: `activate` shouldn't wait on the network, as `annai.net.fetch` has no time limit of its own; and a switch-off by three failures in a run lasts until the user turns the plugin on or annai starts again), the UI's drawings, hooks, a tool's errors, a setup panel's (what it throws counts only when it is a crash, as for a tool), a provider's `list`, a model type's "Errors" and a live protocol's `ready`.
- Why we had to change: a start, a hook and the launch count counted every error but two shapes of a missing link (`isLinkFailure`: the browser's fetch failure, annai's `network` and `unreachable` kinds), and the screens' catches counted every error. Each of annai's own APIs fails in a shape of its own (`annai.llm`'s and `annai.llm.decide`'s `AllFailedError`, a plugin's own timer's `AbortError` on `annai.net.fetch`, a link failure it wrapped in its own `Error`, annai's start limit on a request that waits on a dead link), and each of fail-owner-next's re-reviews found one more shape the list lacked. The design check measured, with the real plugin host, that on main 9 of 15 shapes at a start and 7 of 13 at a hook switched a plugin off without being a crash, and every error at a drawing, a dock action or a component did. A third-party live protocol whose `open()` parsed a typed address that isn't one came out "The plugin for this model's protocol failed.", spared from the count only because the engine's words for a bad URL were in `isBadAddress`'s list; a list's own `new URL` gave the provider page the engine's raw `TypeError`.
- Original design was wrong because: the count asked "is this the link's?" at places where the plugin's code stands between annai's APIs and the catch: it may wrap, swallow or rethrow what annai throws, or crash on the missing reply, and it has links annai never sees (the browser's own `fetch`, a socket). So the answer was a list of shapes, one more for each API a plugin can call. The question the count needs is "is this a crash?", which the six classes the language defines for a program's mistake answer, with the `DOMException` names the engine gives the same mistakes (a bad selector, `atob` on bad input, `structuredClone` of a function: it throws those as a `DOMException`, no class of the six), by an allowlist, as every other name is an answer (a link, a stop, a time limit, a full phone or annai's own store can give `AbortError`, `TimeoutError`, `NetworkError`, `QuotaExceededError`, `InvalidStateError` or `NotFoundError`). No error class annai makes (`AllFailedError`, `ProviderError`, `StorageFullError`, `ValidationError`, `PluginError` and the rest) extends one of them, so nothing annai's own APIs throw for a refusal, a limit or a link can count, now or when an API is added; a crash class the engine throws inside one does, as in the plugin's own code (`annai.files.readJSON` of a file that isn't JSON throws the engine's `SyntaxError`, `annai.files.write` given a `BigInt` or a circular object the `TypeError` of `JSON.stringify`), and annai's own throws of a crash class are for a wrong argument (`toBytes`) and a wrong return (an upload request that gave no request), apart from inflate's `RangeError`s, which `zip.js` turns into a `ZipError` before a plugin sees them. A second rule beside the tool path's was the second copy "2026-11-23 One rule decides whose failure an error is" warned of, two days old when it was removed.
- Routes not taken: marking annai's own link errors where annai makes them (`tracedFetch`, `callRole`), as the endpoint path decides by position (every API a plugin can call would need the mark, `AllFailedError` is also thrown for a refused key, and the browser's own fetch, a wrapped error and annai's start limit stay out of its reach); counting nothing while the device says it is offline (the traveller's link says it is up); no count at a hook or a screen at all (a crashing plugin would never get its card); dropping the launch count (a start that really crashes would never be turned off: kept, counting crashes); logging only what counts (a start's or a hook's error in its own words would then leave no record anywhere, and a plugin page's "the plugin's error is in Trace" would be untrue).
- What it leaves, the plugin's own: a crash set off by a missing or odd reply still counts (a field read of nothing, a sign-in page parsed as JSON), as on the tool path; a start that keeps failing without a crash is never turned off by annai, and its card shows at each launch (proposed as a known limitation, not yet agreed); a start's error outside guard (a module that throws as it loads, a top-level `await` of the network, a storage read) still counts a launch whatever it is; the guide says so for a module and its files, not for a storage read.
- Lesson: where a host runs a plugin's code and the plugin's code stands between the host's own APIs and the catch, count only what the language says is a crash, by its class (and the engine's `DOMException` names for the same mistakes), at one door every such place passes (it takes a throw, and a promise the host awaits; a call whose returned promise the host does not read, such as a live driver's call, a live message handler, an avatar's call or a heard-clips function, gets only its throw caught, and a rejection there counts nothing), and take any other error as the plugin's answer, kept in the trace and counting nothing; a rule of shapes ("not the link's") grows by one for each API the plugin can call. A rule by position fits only where the host makes the request and reads the reply itself. Check a typed address by state before any of the plugin's code builds from it (a `run` type's call and list are left out: it may need no address). This closes the deferred part of "2026-11-23 An endpoint type's failure is decided by where it happened, and annai reads the reply" (annai's own `annai.llm` errors at a start and a hook; a plugin's screen, dock action or avatar counting every error) and replaces its sentence on `isLinkFailure` (a start's, a hook's and the launch count's one rule for "the link's").

## 2026-11-25 A cut live turn ends when the provider's stream says nothing more of it comes
- Changed: the OpenAI Realtime protocol (`plugins-official/openai-realtime`, 0.6.3) emits `{ type: 'turn', end: 'interrupted' }` for a reply it cancels when that reply's `response.done` comes (whatever its status: `cancelled`, or `completed` where the cancel crossed the reply's own end), or at OpenAI's refusal of a `response.create` it had cancelled first, which gets no `response.done` (reasoned from OpenAI's documented protocol, not seen on the real service; the stand-in refuses a create only by script, which reaches this case when a cancel goes out before the refusal comes, and the test feeds that refusal by hand); before, it emitted it inside the cut, as it sent `response.cancel`. A cut with nothing to cancel (the reply all made, her voice still playing by the clock) emits it at once, as before. docs/plugins.md ("Cutting in" and the OpenAI example) now says when a protocol whose server doesn't cut emits it: once nothing more of the cut turn can come, not as it sends the cut. Koe's side, the contract's types and the stand-in are unchanged.
- Why we had to change: remind-first-next's second opinion (2026-11-23, probed with the stand-in): an urgent reminder cut Koe while a card's call of her reply was still on its way. OpenAI sends a cancelled reply's events already made before its `response.done`, as the stand-in and OpenAI's documented order (`response.done` last) have it, so the call came after the protocol's `interrupted`, which had let Koe's cut turn go; the call opened a new, uncut turn, whose card passed the reminder on (`reminder.told`), and nothing showed it, then or at the next open. Gemini's `interrupted` is Gemini's own, and Koe's side was built to expect it after the calls in flight; that order is not measured on the real service (the adapter only forwards Gemini's flag, the Gemini stand-in's cut sends no call between `interrupted` and `turnComplete`, and the known limitation's Revisit asks for a real trace).
- Original design was wrong because: the protocol stood in for a server event OpenAI doesn't send (it has no cut of its own) at the moment it asked for the cut, but Koe takes the provider's turn end as "nothing more of it comes", which only the provider's stream can say: what it sent before the cancel reached it is still on its way. The protocol already waited for that `response.done` before sending anything said after the cut (OpenAI makes one response at a time), so the event that ends the turn was there all along; the end was taken from the wrong side of the wire. That wait is the sender's and old; the reader's is new: Koe's cut turn now stays open until that event comes, so if it never does with the socket up, nothing of the protocol lets the turn go (a drop ends both; the bound a watchdog would give a cut turn is a separate change, not this one, and Gemini's cut turn has the same gap). Koe's own early ends of a turn (`#endNow` for a silent card answered with no sound, `#endLater`'s timer) take "nothing more comes" on Koe's own word, and this change doesn't reach them.
- What it leaves: a call that finds no open turn of hers (the first of the cut reply's events to arrive, or one that comes after a silent card answered with no sound, or a turn's end timer, has ended her turn) still opens a turn of its own and passes the reminder on, as on Gemini: the known limitation "With Koe · Realtime, an urgent reminder can count as told by a card already on its way" (C213), widened to OpenAI Realtime by chief's decision C249 (2026-11-25); the protocol could tell such a call by its cancel being outstanding, but the tool event carries no such word.
- Lesson: an end a reader takes as "nothing more of this comes" is emitted where the source says so (its own end of the thing, or its refusal of it), never where you asked it to stop: what the source had sent before your ask still arrives, and the reader takes it for the next thing. Where nothing more can come (all of it already in hand), end it at once. Hang the end on the event that already holds what is sent after the cut: the sender's wait for it is old, but the reader's is new, so the reader needs its own way out for when the event never comes (a drop ends both; a watchdog must let the turn go). The reader's own early ends (Koe's silent card answered with no sound, its turn's end timer) are its own word for "nothing more comes", and the lesson doesn't reach them.

## 2026-11-26 A typed address's check carries every refusal its request makes
- Changed: `annai.helpers.addressProblem` and `addressShown` (`addressShown` since API 2; the one check every typed provider address passes: a provider's page, an ability's own address, a provider plugin's add, Another address's probe, SearXNG's address, a description's addresses) now refuse an address with a name or password before an `@` (`https://name:password@host/…`), as annai's five other typed-address checks (a backup place's fields, MCP, packages, remote trace, the relay) already did. The contract is the same (null, or why a key can't go to that address); the change adds the one reason it missed, in the words annai's packages check already says in all three languages: "This address holds a name or password before an @. Leave it out." The not-an-address words stopped repeating what was typed, in the same change: one whole sentence, "That isn't a web address: give it with https:// in front.", and Another address's probe stopped rewriting the check's words (it spliced "What you typed" over every copy of the typed text in them, which turned a typed "https://" into "…give it with What you typed in front."; the words never hold the typed text now, so there is nothing to splice).
- Why we had to change: the Deferred entry "A typed address with a user name and password counts as a plugin's bug". Such an address passed the check and was saved, and the first request to it threw (Chromium: "Request cannot be constructed from a URL that includes credentials", with the address in the words; WebKit: "URL is not valid or contains user credentials."): every chat call said "couldn't reach", and a plugin's own fetch on WebKit counted against the plugin. The final review found the words naming an "API key field" that Ollama's page and SearXNG's settings don't have, and a name and password are a Basic sign-in the key field doesn't send: the first words said more than every caller could do, and were cut to "Leave it out.", then to the packages check's own row, which already reads natively in each language. Another review found that the old not-an-address words put the typed text into what SearXNG's `ready()` tells Shiori, so a key pasted into its address field would reach the model.
- Original design was wrong because: the check was written for what annai does with a key (https only, or http for this device), not for what a request can hold. The Fetch standard refuses a name or password in a request's address (measured in Chromium and WebKit, 2026-11-25), and five of the six typed-address checks had learned it one at a time, each in its own words; the one every provider address passes had not, and its builder's first look went to the plugin's own check, one step behind the door. The helpers' answer is a plugin interface, so a plugin that asked "can annai send a key here?" was told yes for an address no browser sends to.
- Lesson: a check of a typed address carries every refusal the request it feeds makes (the Fetch standard's: no name or password); before adding one, find the check every such value already passes, not the plugin's own or one at each place it is typed. Its words never repeat the typed text, which may be a key pasted there by mistake, and name only what every caller has (a field one page lacks is a wrong word on the others).

## 2026-11-26 Largest request (MiB) goes: the token rule and the "too long" catch cover every request it reached
- Changed (size-out-next; the user's answer U141, 2026-11-26: "Take it out (Recommended)"): Settings → Models → Housekeeping's "Largest request (MiB)" is gone, with every part that served it: the field, its range message and three clauses of the Housekeeping sentence (and their drafts in three languages); `compactionNeed`'s `bytes` and `maxBytes` with both their legs (forced over the size, due at the threshold's share of it); `bytesLessFiles` (compaction.js) and `nativeOverlap` (contract.js, which only it called); and the loop's `maxRequestMiB` (its settings, the step loop's forced branch by size, the due check after a reply). The config key `shiori.compaction.maxRequestMiB` stays in stored settings and backups, read as nothing: the loop's settings carry only `threshold`, and nothing is migrated. The recap's photos, which took half of it (`PHOTO_SHARE`), have a room of their own, `PHOTO_ROOM` (4 MiB as base64, the room they had at the field's default), so a recap at the defaults sends what it sent. Housekeeping is the token rule alone: due at the threshold's share of the window or at 100,000 tokens, forced over 90% of the window, and with no window known the "too long" catch (every model refuses the request, housekeeping runs once, the request goes again). The spec's §6.4, §6.5 and §16 and the README's long-conversations line say it.
- Why we had to change: the user, 2026-10-19: "we are an agent on the go possibly with spotty internet. thus we shall make these optimizations when possible"; and 2026-11-12: "what I prefer is simple and robust solutions". Measured 2026-11-25 (the forced-branch design check): since size-forced-next (2026-11-24) the size weighed a request's words only, about 3.5 characters a token, so at its default (8 MiB, about 2.4 million tokens) it never acted: every model in annai's catalog is summarized at 90% of its window (at most about 3.3 MB of words) or refuses the request first. It acted only when set under about 3 MiB, for a model that takes 400,000 tokens or more, in a conversation that never paused for the cache's hour, and there it added summaries (one probe: three in one turn, 4.90 MB sent, the third removing a 10,000-character exchange). Three builds' findings (pdf-refused-next, pdf-again-next, size-forced-next) and a refused PDF at an overflow's brink (closed by llm-rest-next) had all landed on the step loop's forced branch by size.
- Original design was wrong because: the size came with the first design (2026-10-15), when a photo's bytes stayed in every request, so bytes were what a long conversation cost on the link. Once the photo and PDF windows (2026-11-21, 2026-11-22) took files out after two turns, and size-forced-next took the window's files out of what it weighed, it measured what the token rule already measures, the request's words, at a default that came after every catalog model's window. A second limit on one quantity binds only where it is below the first; at a default above it, it decides nothing for anyone who leaves it alone, while its sentence on the Settings screen describes a rule. Each repair since (a flag, a re-based measure) guarded its repeats instead of asking whether it still bound.
- Lesson: when a limit is re-based, measure where it binds against the other rules on the same quantity: one that at its default never acts before another rule takes the same action goes, with its field, its words, its tests and what borrowed its value (which gets a number of its own, with its reason); a stored key that now means nothing is read as nothing, never migrated. API change: the config key `shiori.compaction.maxRequestMiB` is ignored; `compactionNeed` takes `{ tokens, window, threshold }`; `bytesLessFiles` and `nativeOverlap` are gone; recap.js's `PHOTO_SHARE` is now `PHOTO_ROOM`.

## 2026-11-26 The dock says Sending… while a call's photos, PDF or recording go up, told by the call that times the send
- Changed (sending-next; the user's pick U113, 2026-11-26: "The dock says "Sending…""): `callOnce` (src/core/llm/call.js) tells its caller `{ type: 'sending', on }` through `onEvent`, a new annai-only `StreamEvent` (as `restart` is callRole's): on as a photo's upload starts going up (off once the uploads are done), and as a request that sends a photo's, a PDF's or a recording's bytes starts going up, just before its POST with the request built and its address checked (`BYTES`, the one predicate the send's cut words already used), off wherever the send ends: its answer's headers, its failing or its being stopped (callOnce's catch), or the uploads' end by a `finally` in `withUploads`; `take` drops a plugin's own `sending` event, so only callOnce sends it. The loop (`#sendingNow`) only mirrors the events (nothing in it clears the send) and gives Thinking the words Sending… · tap to stop while it is on (`State.sending: true`, `name` still `'thinking'`, so her look, the tap and every reader of the name are as they were); Halves stands `Sending…` over a Koe that rests or waits while Kokoro's own state says it, where it stood `Thinking`. The times are unchanged. `ui.shiori.state()` (docs/plugins.md) carries `sending`.
- Why we had to change: the user's report of 2026-11-20 ("I sent a photo to shiori, and it appears it times out?") and their words of 2026-10-19 ("we are an agent on the go possibly with spotty internet"): since photo-wait-next a request gets the model's wait plus its bytes' time at 70 kbit/s before its answer's headers, so on a dead link a request with one default photo (the request's text about 110 KB, and the photo's 341 KiB as base64), on a link shared with the other half, voice off, said Thinking for 30 s + 2 × 52 s, about 2 min 15 s (82 s on an unshared link), and three models in the chain three times that, about 6 min 45 s, before the card that says the connection may be too slow to send photos or files.
- Original design was wrong because: the dock's Thinking was set by the loop before the call, and the call's two waits (the send, then the model) were known only inside `callOnce`, which timed them: nothing told the caller when the send ended, so the dock named the model for the part of the wait the card then blamed on the link.
- Lesson: a state a person watches through a wait in parts names the part it is in, told as it changes by the code that times the parts, by the same rule its failure's words use, so what the screen said and what the card says agree. Each such send says on at its start and off at its own end, every way it ends (the headers, a failure, a stop), so the reader only mirrors it. Two builds got this wrong: the first told on or off before every request, and four tests that read a call's events as its output (one stops the call at its first event) caught that every words call had gained an event its readers must skip; the second left a failed send unsaid, to be ended by callRole's `restart` before another attempt or by the call's end, and the review found the dock saying Sending… after the request had stopped going up (through the card, and through a reading or a transcription made for the next model), which the loop had been clearing in three places to make up for. The state's name stays, and the new fact is a field beside it, so every reader of the name is unchanged. API change: `StreamEvent` gains `{ type: 'sending', on: boolean }` (only callOnce sends it); the loop's `State` gains `sending?: true` (Halves' too), with the words in `LABELS.sending`.

## 2026-11-26 A heard line nothing can hear is answered by the call that passed every model over
- Changed: `callRole` and `llm.call` take `standIn` beside `askResting`: after every other pass, the models passed over because nothing in the speech-in list could hear a line for them are asked once more, in order, with the line as "[audio, not transcribed]" and the list walked again for each. The turn's own call opts in (Kokoro's always; Koe's when Kokoro's settings give her no ear, "Send audio to the model" not in the speech-in list, which Koe's call and Kokoro's read alike, or no main model that takes audio, loop.js's `#kokoroCanHear`; or when Kokoro's last turn's call failed and no turn of hers has worked since, the record Halves already keeps for the card for both halves, read through the half's `kokoroDown` hook). The speech hook takes its optional parts in one options object, `(model, request, { plain, signal, standIn })`. `Voice.resolver` is back to `(scope, wake, list, shared)`: the clip-typed branch's two Sets (`unheard`, `carried`) went with the loop's `#unheard`, `#carried` and `#pastTurn` and the segment's `Kept.failed`. The function the resolver returns keeps the asks that failed in its call (a transcription model's, for a segment) beside its `cut` set, so a recording a model refused goes up once a call. The write-down's count of tries (`#writeTries`, per Agent) counts a turn's own try only (`#afterTurn`); an unforced housekeeping write-down keeps the sound and counts nothing, and a forced one lets it go at once (`final`).
- Why we had to change: C212 had a heard line nothing could write down fail its own turn with a card, and the clip-typed branch kept that card by marking which lines were past their turn (a Set in the resolver, filled by the loop) and by counting only the asks that were really made (`Kept.failed`). Three review rounds found an edge of "this turn's own line" each time: a Stop before the first request, a voice answer the turn never sent, chatter heard in and outside a turn, a restart with kept clips, the other half's lines. A design check then found that both parts existed only to keep the card, and chief's decision C253 built its route (c1) instead: the turn is answered, and the line goes as not transcribed. The final review of that route found one more shape (Koe on text-only models while Kokoro's audio model can hear the line: Koe answered from the stand-in and the words were never stored, where before Kokoro answered it from its sound and wrote it down); the rule that fixed it, by capability alone, failed in another when Kokoro's only audio model rested (out of credit, a stated long rest, a rate limit that keeps coming back): Koe's call failed and every line typed after the heard one failed with it, as long as the model rested, where before Koe answered them. The next rule, a forecast from the bench of whether Kokoro's call could ask that model now, failed again once the rest ran out (lines typed minutes apart: out of credit rests 3 minutes), and from the first line for a model that refuses each request with a 400, which rests nothing. A second design check found the forecast was the part to remove: Koe now reads how Kokoro's last turn's call ended (route (d)). That is still a guess, since Koe answers before Kokoro's call for the line runs, but one her next turn that worked corrects. Its cost, found by a probe in the unit kit (three heard lines said while a turn of Kokoro's took 10 s): while her model works again and no turn of hers has worked since, Koe answers each line said as not heard, and the first can be given up unwritten at Koe's third turn that worked, since Koe's turns count the tries and Kokoro's turn writes only while Koe is down. The final review also found a refused recording going up again in every walk of the list and in the last pass, and a count that housekeeping's write-down fed (a line given up after one turn that worked and a quiet spell).
- Original design was wrong because: it decided per turn, with state across calls, what one call can decide from its own attempts. Every boundary of "the turn's own line" was another rule, and once a line was marked the capability-gap skip was lost too (a text-only model answered while a resting audio model behind it was never asked). The count of real asks needed every kind of failure sorted into asks that count, and each round found another class. The same mistake in small, at the call's own level: a flag on the stored segment for what only the call knows (that this ask already failed here). And Koe's opt-in guessed, from the bench, what Kokoro's later call would do; the bench knows only the past, and a model whose rest has run out, or that rests nothing, reads as one that will answer, so each wrong guess failed a line. Reading how her last turn ended is a guess too, but one read from an outcome, which her next turn that worked clears, never from a record a clock ends.
- What it leaves: the card "Nothing in the speech-in list worked for …" no longer shows for a heard line its turn answers, so nothing on screen points at Settings → Models for a transcription model that fails without resting (the Deferred entry "With route (c1), nothing on screen names the transcription model that can't hear", docs/limitations.md); with a hanging transcription model each line not yet written down adds one wait to the turn's end (its own Deferred entry).
